It is now bounded with `FullMode.Wait`, so the Gateway applies backpressure instead of buffering; when the channel stays full past a 5 second timeout the command is refused with `WORKER_COMMAND_QUEUE_FULL`, carrying `sentToWorker: false` and `retryable: true` because the command was never enqueued and a retry therefore cannot duplicate its effect. The refusal decrements the queued-command counter, which is incremented before the write is attempted, so a refusal no longer leaks queue depth for a command that never arrived. Both transports are covered by the one bound: the direct-pipe write and the shared-host broker relay are alternatives inside the single writer loop that drains it, and a structural guard pins that so a second unbounded path cannot be added silently ([#369](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/369)).
- Cancelling a command that is still queued now releases its capacity immediately and guarantees it never runs. A cancel that overtook its command on the pipe was recorded only as a pre-cancellation, so the command kept its slot and its bytes until it reached the head of the queue, where it started with an already-cancelled token. Capacity was not released at cancellation time, and whether the cancelled work happened to do anything depended on each individual handler observing the token. Queued items now carry the command's `cancelToken`, and `Control:Cancel` removes them from the queue under the scheduler lock, refunding count, bytes and per-client accounting ([#369](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/369)).
- `genexus_analyze mode=callers` can actually be paged. The Worker already accepted a `cursor`, `maxCallers` and `maxSourceBytes` and answered with a resumable `scan.nextCursor`, but `AnalyzeRouter` forwarded only `target`/`type` and `CommandDispatcher` called `FindCallerSites(target)` with defaults, so a high-fan-in target always returned the first page - and because `genexus_analyze` already declares a generic `cursor` for other modes, a caller following the hint sent `cursor=N` and it was silently dropped, returning the same page forever. The cursor and both budgets are now forwarded and declared in the schema. The scan also always makes progress: the first caller of a page is attempted even when it alone overruns a budget (the budget refused its first unit, so a page could stop with `callersScanned=0` and a `nextCursor` equal to its input - a livelock), and a caller that cannot be processed is listed in `scan.skippedCallers` with a reason instead of being dropped, so a skipped caller is never read as one with no call sites. A page either advances its cursor or reports `scan.complete: true`. The published tool profile budget moves 82500 -> 83000 bytes for the two new arguments ([#366](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/366)).
- Shared-host supervision now describes the Worker it is supervising, and can actually elect a recycle. The broker built its `heartbeat_ack` supervision block by calling `Program.GetSdkBusyStatus()` inside the broker process, which reads process statics - but the broker starts a separate child process and only relays frames to it, so the broker's own scheduler is always empty and its SDK lane always idle. Every attachment therefore saw `sdkBusy=false`, `sdkState=idle` and a zero queue depth next to the child's PID even while that child was deadlocked. The lane state now comes from a snapshot captured from the child and forwarded with its capture time; a missing or stale snapshot reports `available:false` with a reason instead of idle defaults, so "we did not look" cannot read as "nothing is happening". The managed-heap figures were `GC.GetTotalMemory` in the broker reported next to the child's PID; they are replaced by the child's own report (`childManagedHeapBytes` with `childManagedHeapSource`), and the two forced blocking gen-2 collections that ran on every acknowledgement, per attachment, on the process routing every client's traffic are gone from the heartbeat path. Finally, `_recycleElected` was only ever written with `0` and the Gateway acts on a shared Worker solely when the acknowledgement reports it true, so heap-pressure and stall recovery for shared Workers could never happen: the broker now evaluates the policy against the child snapshot and raises the election once per generation with `Interlocked.CompareExchange`, with progressing work and a non-empty queue blocking recovery. **Partial:** the broker-owned probe that *acquires* that child snapshot is not implemented yet - until it is, a production broker has nothing feeding `RecordChildStatus` and so reports `available:false` for the lane, which is the safe direction (it no longer claims a healthy idle child) but is not yet the supervision the issue asked for ([#362](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/362)).
- A warm reopen of the source store no longer reads every stored body. On catalog load the store read, decompressed and hash-checked each record in parallel to rebuild the trigram index, so Worker startup I/O scaled with the whole store on every start even when nothing had changed. The derived postings (the per-trigram key sets and each record's own set, which #344 needs for reclamation) are now persisted beside the catalog, stamped with the catalog's own SHA-256 digest and record count. They are used only when both match; a missing, stale, partial or corrupt file falls back to the full rebuild, so search results are identical either way and no false negative can be introduced. Certifications stay in memory, so a reopen still starts uncertified and the first coverage probe validates bodies lazily ([#374](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/374)).
- The lite-index walk no longer rewrites its whole accumulated snapshot on a fixed interval. `WriteLiteWalkCheckpoint` serializes everything walked so far, and it ran every 2000 objects, so a walk of N objects wrote about `sum(k*C*s) = O(N^2/C)` bytes - on a KB with tens of thousands of objects, many times the final index size, all on the indexing path. The interval now doubles after each write (2000, 4000, 8000, ...), which bounds the total at O(N) with O(log N) writes; the price is more rework after an interruption, which the existing resume semantics already tolerate because a checkpoint is re-walkable work rather than a correctness boundary. A resume deep into a walk continues the doubling instead of re-checkpointing the same state, and the interval is capped so a very long walk cannot overflow ([#373](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/373)).
- `genexus_inspect` no longer reads live SDK objects off the owner thread. The five sections (`signature`, `variables`, `structure`, `domainsAndEnums`, `callers`) were `Task.Run` lambdas holding the live `KBObject` and calling `GetParametersInternal`, `PartAccessor.GetVariableObjects`, part source reads, `_kbService.GetKB()` and reference walks from thread-pool threads while the owner STA sat in `Task.WaitAll` - the GeneXus object model is not thread-safe, so those reads raced the thread that owns them. Each section now captures on the thread already running the request, into its own slot, and the slots are merged on that same thread. That also closes the late-writer problem #334 left open: a section that overran the budget kept running and could still write into an envelope that had already been returned and cached, so a response could carry both `incompleteSections: ["callers"]` and a `callers` key; the deadline is now checked between sections and inside the reference walk, so no background work outlives the request. A section that throws is reported in `sectionErrors` instead of being wrapped in `catch {}`, so "this object has no callers" stays distinguishable from "the caller walk failed" ([#368](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/368)).
- `PromoteSourceForSearch` no longer re-sums every indexed source on each promotion. The aggregate `FullSource` budget check looped over `index.Objects.Values` and summed `FullSource.Length` for every indexed object each time one more source was considered, so promoting S sources cost O(S x N) character-length reads across the index - quadratic while populating a cold KB, and paid even far below the 8 MiB / 256 MiB character budgets. A running total is now kept, reconciled with one pass wherever the index object itself is swapped (load, restore, replace, checkpoint), and kept in step when a body is promoted, replaced or cleared. The check and the write now happen under the same lock as the entry, so two concurrent promotions cannot both pass the budget. Budget semantics are unchanged; a source-shape guard keeps the per-call sum from coming back ([#364](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/364)).
- `genexus_logs` filters (`grep`, `filterCorrelation`, `objectFilter`, `since`) again reach the whole log instead of only the last `lines` lines. Moving `ReadLogs` off the SDK lane in #342 was right, but the bounded tail read changed what the filters mean: the retained window was filtered *afterwards*, so `lines=10, grep="X"` became "the last 10 lines that contain X" instead of "the last 10 matching lines from anywhere in the file" - a diagnostic search for a correlation id from a few minutes ago came back empty on a busy log. The predicates are now applied to each complete line as it is decoded while scanning backwards, so a match anywhere in the scanned range is found and retained memory stays bounded by the number of matches returned. `since=<timestamp>` stops the scan at the first older line, and `since=crash` walks back to the marker, which makes a crash older than the old 2000-line window findable. A scan stopped by the new byte budget (`GXMCP_LOG_SCAN_MAX_MB`, default 256 MiB) reports `scanComplete:false` with `scannedBytes` and a hint instead of implying absence, and `totalLines` reports the file's line count rather than the window size ([#370](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/370)).
- A busy SDK lane that has never emitted a progress notification is now recoverable. The progress marker is reset per command and only set by `ProgressEmitter`, so only builds and bulk indexing ever set it; most SDK calls - a single object read, a save, an inspect, or a COM call blocked behind a modal dialog - emit none. Those stayed `busy-unproven` for as long as they lasted, and since only `busy-stalled` is a recovery target, a deadlocked SDK that reports nothing was never recovered by `genexus_connection_recover` or by shared-host supervision (which applied the same rule). Past a new configurable grace period (`GXMCP_SDK_NO_PROGRESS_STALL_MS`, default 10 minutes) a lane is classified `busy-stalled-unproven` and becomes a recovery candidate; the diagnosis carries `sdkStallReason` (`no-progress-ceiling` or `progress-stopped`) and `sdkNoProgressStallAfterMs`. A short busy call is still left alone, and a lane that has proved it reports progress is still `busy-progressing` for any elapsed time. The broker's copy of the rule now takes the elapsed time too, so the two cannot disagree about the same child ([#371](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/371)).
- Multi-KB discovery (`genexus_query kbs=[...]`) no longer reports a paged KB as complete. `DispatchOneKbAsync` set `Complete = true` for every successful per-KB response, so an entry carried `complete=true` next to `hasMore=true` and the federation's `coverage.complete` could be `true` while results were missing; completeness is now derived from the absence of a `nextCursor` and from a Worker-reported truncated page. A budget cut also no longer hands out a cursor that skips what it trimmed: the Worker's cursor points past the whole page it returned, so the items dropped from that page were unreachable, and the entry now reports `droppedByBudget` + `budgetCut.resumeSkipsPageRemainder` and withdraws the cursor instead of claiming the rest is resumable. Each leg also re-checks the Worker its plan captured and answers `notOpen` rather than dispatching through the ordinary acquiring path, which could spawn a KB - and evict the one the caller was working in - for a KB the plan had already found closed ([#377](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/377)).
- A `notifications/resources/updated` frame that reaches the Gateway without a KB alias no longer wipes every open KB's cache. The blank alias mapped to the empty scope, which `SemanticCacheStore.InvalidateScope` treats as "clear everything": it called `Clear()`, so one unattributable notification - a Worker whose alias was not propagated, or the legacy single-worker path - discarded every other KB's entries *and* reset their scope revisions to zero, which is the exact "subscriber cannot tell anything moved" failure #336 was opened for, reintroduced globally. The unattributable path now uses `InvalidateEveryKnownScope`, which advances each known scope and removes every entry (still correct: a change that cannot be attributed to one KB is not safe to attribute to a narrower one) while keeping every revision monotonic, and the miss is logged and counted in `UnattributedResourceUpdates` instead of being silent ([#379](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/379)).
- A `genexus_read` carrying `ifUnchangedSince` is never answered from the Gateway semantic cache. The Worker side of a conditional read is conservative - it recomputes the object's revision stamp and only omits the body when the token still matches - but the cache sat in front of it keyed on the arguments, so a request that once produced `notModified` was replayed for its whole 30-minute TTL without the Worker being consulted. An edit made outside this Gateway that produced no resource-updated notification therefore left a stale "unchanged" in place and an agent polling with the same token never saw the change. A TTL is not evidence that an external edit did not happen, so a conditional read is now excluded from both the lookup and the store (a null key does both); unconditional reads keep their caching and hit rate, and a cached unconditional read keeps handing back the `contentToken` of the revision it actually served, which stops matching and forces the Worker check ([#376](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/376)).
- The first-touch warm pass is now claimed against the KB it is actually warming. The scope key resolved its alias from `Environment.DefaultKb` and then "whichever KB is open", never from the KB context the index bootstrap had just set, so with a default of `KbAlpha` the bootstrap for `KbBeta` computed `KBALPHA|<Alpha generation>`: if Alpha had already warmed, Beta's pass was skipped. This is the #340 defect reappearing in the multi-KB case that fix was meant to address. The resolver now takes the KB explicitly, then the ambient KB context, and only falls back to the configured default for a caller that genuinely has no KB context ([#365](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/365)).
- Source-store eviction no longer lets the tracked byte total drift above the catalog. `EnforceStorageBudget` subtracted an evicted record's bytes only from its local loop variable, so `_trackedBytes` kept every evicted byte forever: each later `Put` read a total that was still over budget, evicted again, and the store drained progressively while every insert paid a full catalog sort. Eviction now applies the delta once the record has left `_records` (whichever way the file delete goes, since the catalog no longer tracks those bytes either), a failed delete records an orphaned file instead of marking the whole counter untrusted on every failure, and `TrackedBytesMatchCatalog`/`OrphanedFileCount` expose the invariant. A settled store now oscillates between its 85% high-water mark and the budget instead of collapsing ([#363](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/363)).
- `LiteEntryAccumulator` no longer corrupts the lite index after a resume checkpoint that held a duplicated GUID. The duplicate-GUID fallback and `Compact()` both physically remove elements, which shifts every later element left, but neither rebuilt the GUID -> slot map: a later `Upsert` then wrote its tombstone at a pre-removal index. With one trailing entry the stale index silently nulled the freshly appended replacement (one object vanished from the index, another appeared twice); with enough elements removed it threw `ArgumentOutOfRangeException`. Both paths now reindex after the removal (the constructor shares the same helper), the output stays bit-identical to the pre-#337 `RemoveAll`-then-append behaviour, and the unique-GUID fast path keeps its constant per-entry cost. A `SlotMapIsConsistent` invariant and a randomized property test against a `RemoveAll` reference now cover the ordering ([#378](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/378)).
- `genexus_variable` `modify` no longer fails with `UnknownType` when only `description` is passed: the new `description` argument updates (or, with an empty string, clears) the Description in place and keeps the variable's type, binding and shape; `modify` with neither a type nor a description now returns `MissingParameter` instead of `UnknownType`. `description` now also applies to a `BusinessComponent` variable: `objectType=BusinessComponent` previously accepted the argument, returned success and changed nothing, because the Business Component path never received it.
- `genexus_transfer action=import dryRun=false confirm=true` refused every XPZ that contained a Procedure with `TransferImportVerificationUnavailable: The XPZ contains a WebForm candidate without a readable raw WebForm payload`, even for a package the MCP had just exported from an unchanged Procedure, with or without a print block. The import fidelity check added for issue #102 (WebForm `GxWidth`/`AttID` loss) asks `WebFormXmlHelper.GetWebFormPart` for each object's visual part, and that helper deliberately also returns a Procedure's report `Layout` part so `ObjectService` can read it as visual XML. Every Procedure therefore counted as a WebForm candidate, but the XPZ serializes that layout as a direct `<Layout>` child of `<Part>` rather than as `<Source>` CDATA, so `ReadExportWebForms` found no payload and the check failed closed. Report and layout parts (`ReportLayoutHelper.IsReportPartTypeName`) are no longer WebForm fidelity candidates; real WebForm parts of Transactions and WebPanels are unchanged and still fail closed when their raw payload is missing. `GetWebFormPart` itself is untouched, so its other callers are unaffected.
- `genexus_read` now accepts `objectName` as an alias of `name` (the spelling `genexus_search_source` uses) instead of silently dropping it and answering `Object not found:` with an empty name plus unrelated "Did you mean" suggestions; a read with no object identity at all now fails fast with a `usage_error` and performs no lookup.
- `genexus_edit` with `patch={find,replace}` and no `mode` wrote the part empty and reported success. The Gateway only entered the patch branch when `mode == "patch"`; with `mode` absent the call fell through to the full-write branch, which reads only `content`, so a `patch`-only call persisted an empty string (`requested.length` 0, hash of the empty string) over the existing source. This is the same silent data loss the `operation` guard closed for issue #43, through a different carrier. A supplied `patch` now implies `mode=patch` (object, string and RFC 6902 array forms), and `patch` combined with `mode=full`, `ops` or `xml` is a `usage_error` instead of a discarded patch. A full-part write with no `content` key (absent or `null`) is now rejected with `ContentRequired` before anything is sent to the Worker; an explicit `content=""` is still accepted as an intentional clear.
- **`genexus_properties action=set propertyName=idBasedOn` on an Attribute now applies the Domain instead of returning `PropertyApplied` without writing anything.** `idBasedOn` is the SDK name GeneXus shows for an Attribute's "Based on", but `IsDomainPropertyName` only knew `Domain`, `DomainBasedOn`, `BasedOn` and `DomainDefinition`, so the write fell into the generic scalar setter, which cannot bind a string to a `BasedOnReference` and fails silently. The post-save check then could not read the property back (unverifiable), so the plain `PropertyApplied` carried no `before`/`persisted` block and a following `get` still showed an empty `idBasedOn`. `idBasedOn` now takes the same typed Domain path as `propertyName=Domain` (existence check, `DomainBasedOn` write, persisted-name verification). A value qualified as `Domain:<Name>` is accepted on this path, because the SDK qualified-name lookup could not resolve the prefixed form; an `Attribute:<Name>` value still fails with `DomainNotFound`. `get` aliasing is unchanged: `idBasedOn` is excluded from the Domain read alias, so `get Domain` keeps returning the domain name and `get idBasedOn` still returns its own entry.
- An in-process build whose specification failed no longer reports `Succeeded` with 0 errors. Inside the Worker, GeneXus emits specifier and generator diagnostics as `>O1<code>: <message>|<SourcePosition>` (error) and `>O2<code>: ...` (warning). Those lines carry no `error`/`warning` word, so `HandleLine` never counted them. A procedure failing with `spc0010` (type mismatch) or `spc0167` came back with an empty `ErrorsDetailed`. The lines are now normalized to `error spc0010: <message> [Object, line N]` before the existing parsers see them, so they are counted, itemized and reach `SpecificationDiagnostics`. Separately, BuildOne keeps going after `>E0Specification`: it compiles the target from its previously generated `.cs`, then fails in WebAppConfig, and the runner accepted that as "compile OK, post-compile failed → partial success", shipping a DLL that was not the requested source. A failed Specification or Generation section now rules out partial success. With `specifyOnly`, a spec pass that closed a section with `E0` but produced no itemized line is reported as a spec failure, not as "could not run in-process".
- `genexus_lifecycle action=specify` no longer specifies nothing for a Transaction that shares its name with its Table, or for a `Type:Name` target. The spec-check passed the raw names to `SpecifyOneOnly`, which resolves them with `ObjectNameHelper.GetKey`; that returns null for both shapes, so GeneXus logged "object not found", ran an empty Specification and the call reported success. The targets are now resolved with the same homonym-safe `ResolveTargetKBObject` the build path uses, and the Specifier is called with those keys. A spec-check where no target resolves now fails with an itemized error instead of succeeding.
- Multi-target builds with `includeCallees=none` now report the specifier diagnostics of each target. `BuildWithTheseOnly` and the verified `Build` overload are GenexusBLServices calls made outside an MSBuild task, so their `>O1`/`>O2` output went to `CommonServices.Output` with no listener, and a failing batch only said "compiler diagnostics were not captured on this route". These calls, and the spec-check above, now run inside a task's `OutputSubscribe`/`OutputUnsubscribe` pair, so their output reaches the build status; the `NativeBuildFailed` message no longer claims diagnostics were lost when some were itemized.
- A spec-check of an object edited since the last build now warns that the result is stale. GeneXus specifies the environment's copy of an object, and only a build refreshes that copy (`CopyModel`, after the reorganization check), so `specify` validated the last built source: it repeated an error that had already been fixed, and passed an error that had just been introduced. Copying the model from the spec-check could hide a pending reorganization, so this change does not do that; it compares the design and environment `LastUpdate` of each target and says so in `newWarnings`.
- `genexus_structure action=update_group` now saves each relinked subtype Attribute, so the `subtypeOf` link persists. It previously returned `GroupUpdated` with `persistedVerified:true` while every member read back as its own supertype and `IS_SUBTYPE=False`; the post-save verification now re-reads each member's supertype and reports `GroupUpdateNotPersisted` with `wrongSupertype` on a mismatch.
- `genexus_layout action=add_report_control` with a `caption` no longer reports `ReportControlWriteVerificationFailed` ("The SDK changed the report control caption during save") for a label whose caption persisted unchanged: the read-back carries the text as `Caption` while the write sets `Text`, and the verification now accepts either spelling.
- `genexus_read part=WebForm` no longer serves the pre-write body after a `genexus_layout` WebForm write. The write marked only the `Layout` read-cache entry dirty, while the body is also cached under `WebForm`; a WebForm write now drops every cached part of the object.
- `genexus_layout` `set_property` / `set_properties` on a report layout no longer silently change only the first of several same-named controls (e.g. the same label in two print blocks); they now fail with `AmbiguousControl`, listing each match's print block and path, and accept the get_tree path (`/Report/PrintBlock[2]/Control[1]`) as `control` to disambiguate.
- Multi-KB discovery (`kbs`) no longer throws away the results of the KBs that already finished when the caller cancels while another KB is still queued. A queued KB's cancellation used to fail the whole fan-out instead of returning a `canceled` outcome for that KB, so `Task.WhenAll` discarded every completed result. A KB whose slot is granted after the cancel is no longer dispatched either.
- Structure DSL `Attr : DomainName` now bases a new attribute on that Domain. The Domain lookup used a signature the SDK does not have, so it always missed and the attribute silently got the default `NUMERIC(4)`. The write is also no longer reported as `WriteNotPersisted`: the Structure re-read renders a domain-based attribute with its physical type (`CHARACTER(10)`), so post-save verification now treats `Attr : SomeDomain` as matching only when the persisted attribute is really based on that domain.
- Terse error envelopes stopped dropping the reason of an SDK refusal. Only the first line of an error message was kept, so the reasons the SDK writes as `* reason` lines under a header that ends in `:` were lost (for example `genexus_delete_object` on an attribute an index still uses reported only "Object(s) could not be deleted:"). Those bullet lines are now kept on the same line; stack traces and other trailing lines are still dropped.
- `genexus_delete_object` on a Transaction left the objects the SDK deletes in cascade (its Table and the Table's Indexes) in the search index, so `list_objects`/`query` kept returning them and every read failed with `IndexedObjectUnavailable`. The result now reports what the SDK removed in cascade as `alsoDeleted` (`{name,type,guid}` list), and those objects are dropped from the index once the deletion is verified, so a later `list_objects`/`query` no longer lists an object that cannot be read.
- `genexus_transfer action=import` run as a preview (dryRun omitted or true) now says so: the response carries `previewOnly`, `imported:false`, a `hint` and a `nextAction` with `dryRun:false` + `confirm:true`, plus a `warning` when `confirm:true` was passed without `dryRun:false` (confirm alone does not import). The dryRun default is unchanged.
- `genexus_layout set_property` no longer leaves a rewritten control behind when its read-back misses. A Caption set on a default Transaction `gxButton` (identified by `ControlName`, no `id`) was committed, the SDK renamed the control and dropped its `Event`/`Class`, and the read-back answered a bare `LayoutReadBackFailed` without undoing anything. A typed write whose verification fails is now rolled back from the pre-write XML and the rollback is checked with the same lookup the write used, so the error reports `rolledBack`, `rollbackVerified`, `missingAfterSave` and `appearedAfterSave` (a rename shows up as one of each) instead of leaving a damaged control in the form.
- `genexus-mcp doctor`, `status` and `config show` now fall back to `~/.genexus-mcp/config.json` outside a KB folder, as the Gateway and the zero-config launcher already do. Without `GX_CONFIG_PATH` or a `config.json` in the current directory they reported "GX config file is missing" while the runtime was loading the user-profile config; doctor now reports it as `source: neutral`. The fallback is read-only: `clients add` and `config migrate` still resolve only `GX_CONFIG_PATH` or a `config.json` in the current directory, because both write to the path they resolve — `clients add` patches it and `config migrate` reads it as `--from` — and silently rewriting the shared user profile from any folder is not a fallback either command should have. Use `GX_CONFIG_PATH` to point `clients add` at a specific config.
- `genexus-mcp init` (and the zero-config launcher in a KB folder) no longer picks the newest GeneXus update of the KB major when the `.gxw` names an older one: with several installs of the same major it now prefers the install whose `major.minor.update` matches the KB version (e.g. U7 for an `18.0.7.x` KB), falling back to the previous same-major choice. Opening a KB with a newer update converts it.
- `genexus_properties` now reads and sets a WebPanel's `MasterPage` (a `WebPanelReference`) by object name: `get` returns the referenced master page name (empty when none) instead of the CLR type name, `set` resolves the name, writes the reference, verifies it on re-read and clears it with an empty value; an unknown name returns `ReferencedObjectNotFound` and writes nothing. A read-only `WebPanelReference` property is still refused with `PropertyReadOnly`, and a property value that is not a reference keeps rendering its own value.
- `SourceStoreFreshnessCertificationTests.A_Corrupted_Body_On_Disk_Is_Not_Reported_As_Certified` is no longer flaky on `main`. The test rewrote the stored body at the same length and then called `File.SetLastWriteTimeUtc(file, DateTime.UtcNow)` to change the file identity; on net48 `UtcNow` advances in ~15.6 ms steps, so a warm-JIT body finishing in 3-4 ms often restored the exact mtime the certification had recorded, and the test observed a correctly-reported `true` (10 runs of the class: 4 pass / 6 fail). The stamp is now moved by a fixed one second from the file's own timestamp, which keeps the test's intent and makes it deterministic. The underlying trade-off is unchanged: certifications are keyed on size and mtime, so a same-size rewrite inside one clock tick is undetectable by design ([#390](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/390)).
- **`genexus_edit` can now write a Data Selector's conditions (`part=Conditions`).** `genexus_read` returned structured conditions but `genexus_edit part=Conditions` failed with `Part 'Conditions' not found in DataSelector`. `part=Conditions mode=full` now replaces the condition list (one condition per line, optional trailing `;`), leaves parameters/orders/Defined By untouched, and verifies the saved conditions by re-reading them (`WriteNoChange` when identical, `WriteNotPersisted` on mismatch). A Data Selector whose conditions sit inside a nested AND/OR group is refused with `DataSelectorConditionsNestedGroupsUnsupported` and nothing is saved, because the one-per-line form carries no grouping and replacing the list would otherwise flatten it silently. `genexus_read parts=["conditions"]` also returns the same text as `source`, and `Conditions` is listed in the part hint.

### Internal

- Cleared the 27 nullable/unused-variable warnings the worker-liveness classifier and shared WorkerHost changes introduced, so the Release warning baseline passes without a baseline update.

### Internal

- The contract-inventory generator reads the classifier's policy by field name instead of by formatting. `generate-operation-contract-inventory.py` located all six declarations through literal text: `private static readonly HashSet<string> <Field>`, a closing `\n        };` at exactly eight spaces, and the `// Only actions` comment that happens to follow `ActionContracts` today. Those are properties of the current formatting, not of the policy, and none of them is stated anywhere the next person edits - so re-indenting the file, dropping a modifier, or letting an editor's brace preference through broke a published artifact at release time, with a reformat as the trigger and a `ValueError` in the release gate as the only signal. Each declaration is now found by its name in the leading token run of a line followed by `=`, and its body ends at the brace that balances the initialiser's opening brace; comments and string literals are consumed as opaque units so a brace inside either cannot move the boundary, and the entry pattern inside `ActionContracts` tolerates whitespace the same way. Failing closed is unchanged and is the point: a declaration that is genuinely absent still raises naming the field and the file, because a tolerant parse that returned an empty set on a rename would publish an inventory silently missing every tool in that class. `DryRunCapableActions` no longer carries its own private copy of the same search, so there is one reader rather than two that must be kept in agreement. Three tests pin it in the Python lane, which is the lane that sees this and the .NET suite does not: one asserts all six declarations resolve and are non-empty, one runs the reader over a re-indented and modifier-stripped copy of the real source and requires an identical result, and one deletes each declaration in turn and requires a `ValueError` rather than an empty set. The reformatting test is the durable one - the tolerant match is exactly the code someone who means well will tighten back, and without it that tightening is invisible until a release fails. No published output changes: the regenerated inventory is byte-identical. The policy stays in `OperationClassifier.cs`, where `Describe` already consumes it, and the obvious next step - having C# emit the policy as JSON so there is no parse at all - is not taken here.

### Added

- An agent re-reading a part it already holds can now learn that nothing changed without paying for the bytes again. A single-part `genexus_read` returns an opaque `contentToken`; echo it back as `ifUnchangedSince` and a provably unchanged representation answers `notModified: true` with identity, freshness metadata and no `source` — while the `versionToken` stays usable as a write's `baseVersion`, because the revision that gated the suppression has not moved. Every other outcome returns the complete body plus a fresh token and `conditional.reason` naming the check that failed (`revisionAdvanced`, `objectReplaced`, `partChanged`, `paginationChanged`, `kbChanged`, `modelChanged`, `workerRestarted`, `revisionUnknown`, `tokenMalformed`, `authoritativeReadRequired`). The freshness evidence is an SDK object revision stamp observed *before* the body is read — deliberately not a TTL and not a cache hit, because both keep claiming "unchanged" after an external IDE edit. A missed signal, a token bound to another KB/model/Worker/object/part/window, and an outstanding post-write recovery fence all degrade to a full read; there is no "probably unchanged" branch, because that is the one answer a caller cannot act on. `ifUnchangedSince` is rejected with `ConditionalReadUnsupportedForm` on `targets[]`, `parts[]` and full-object reads, which have no single representation to bind, and omitting it leaves the conditional read exactly as it was. Fixes [#357](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/357).

- Locating an object across KBs is now one call instead of one call per KB with a cursor to merge by hand. `genexus_query` accepts `kbs: [...]` and runs the same read-only search over that explicit set in one bounded operation, so same-named objects in separate KBs keep their own GUID, path and KB attribution instead of collapsing into a flat name. What earns the right to claim coverage is being explicit about what it did not do: each KB carries its own `status` and `complete` flag, and the envelope carries a `coverage` summary — a KB that is not open, still warming, slow, or failing is reported individually and never as zero matches. It never opens a KB implicitly, because the Worker pool has finite capacity and an implicit open can evict the KB you are working in; a not-open KB says so and names the command that opens it. Bounds are real, not advisory: at most 16 aliases, `maxConcurrency` (default 4, max 8), a per-KB deadline, and `maxTotalResults` across all KBs — overflow marks the affected KB incomplete with its `nextCursor` intact instead of quietly returning a short answer that looks whole. Per-KB continuation is `cursors: {alias: nextCursor}`, and a cursor for a KB outside `kbs` is rejected rather than dropped, because a silently discarded cursor is how a resumed page repeats its first page. No new search engine: each leg runs the existing `Search -> Query` route, so "a match" means the same thing federated as it does alone, and no cross-KB call graph is implied. Scoped to `genexus_query`, as the issue proposes for a first step: source-reference federation via the bounded source search, and `genexus_list_objects`, can follow once per-item token and budget semantics are explicit — neither is implied here. Fixes [#356](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/356).

- The tool-schema budgets move 33500 → 34000 tokens and 82000 → 82500 bytes for the `all` profile, for the two features above: `genexus_read.ifUnchangedSince` and `genexus_query`'s `kbs`/`cursors`/`maxConcurrency`/`maxTotalResults`/`perKbTimeoutMs` land at ~33890 tokens / 82032 bytes. Both descriptions were trimmed while being written. The part that would not compress is the coverage wording, because an agent fanning out across KBs has to be told which KBs were actually searched: "KbBeta timed out" and "KbBeta has no match" call for completely different next moves and cost the same two words to render.

### Fixed

- The wedged-shutdown reap is broker-gated like its two siblings, and the false nullability on `Classify`'s third parameter is gone. The entry below deliberately deferred adding `mayRecycle` to the wedged branch; that deferral is now overturned, because the reason it was safe is exactly what the null-dereference fix removed. Issue #335's rule is that a shared Gateway may act only once the broker reports an election, and `mayRecycle` carries that. The idle and heap reaps have carried it since #335. The wedged branch did not, and only by accident: the log line interpolated `pid={_process.Id}`, a shared Gateway owns no child `Process` so `_process` is null, the throw happened inside the interpolated string, and the loop's own `catch (Exception)` swallowed it before the `StopProcess(WorkerStopReason.Wedged)` on the next line could run - so the gate was never load-bearing for as long as the crash stood in front of it. Removing the crash made the branch reachable with two Gateways attached to one Worker, each free to decide to kill it, which is the two-watchers-one-Worker reap #335 exists to prevent. **What changes in `shared-host` mode**: a wedged Worker is reaped only by the Gateway the broker elected. A Gateway that observes the same wedge without an election logs `worker_wedged_observed` and stops there; it re-reads `recycleElected` on the next pass and reaps if the broker elects it in the meantime, because `SupervisionCanRecycle` is evaluated per pass against live supervision state with nothing latched and nothing lost. The token is deliberately not `worker_wedged_shutdown`, which is the string an operator greps for to establish that a reap happened - reusing it would let a second watcher's line stand in as the elected Gateway's confirmation, and two Gateways watching one wedge would leave that grep ambiguous rather than wrong. The branch also gained an `else`: before the gate, the `continue` above made the "still emitting output" line unreachable once the silence ceiling was crossed, and a non-elected Gateway falling through to it would have reported a Worker silent past the ceiling as progressing. Both the gate and the log split are covered by source-shape guards in the Gateway suite, labelled as source-shape because the decision and its effect are separated by a live Worker process, a running pipe, two Gateway processes on one broker and 15-second timer passes; five mutations were run against them (gate deleted, gate emptied with the reap left outside it, `else` flattened, the non-elected line renamed to `worker_wedged_shutdown`, and the idle sibling's gate dropped) and each turned the guard red. **Not verified end to end:** the live gate this repo requires for Worker supervision changes - two Gateways attached to one shared broker with a deliberately wedged Worker - cannot be run here, so this is review and source-shape coverage only. The change is an improvement on what shipped, not a new behaviour: in `shared-host` a wedged Worker was previously never reaped at all, because the null dereference aborted the call before `StopProcess` and the loop's own catch swallowed the throw. Isolated mode is unaffected - it owns a child `Process`, so `_process` was never null there and only the log token changed (`pid=` to `id=`, matching the two siblings).
- `WorkerLivenessClassifier.Classify`'s `sdkProbe` parameter was annotated non-nullable while the body null-checks it in its first branch, the XML doc said "or null when the probe did not return at all", the only production caller declares `JObject sdkProbe = null` and assigns `null` again in a `catch`, and a test pins `null` as a legitimate input. The annotation was false, and that cost more than the diagnostics it produced: a non-nullable parameter switches off nullable flow analysis across the boundary, so a null arriving from a call site added later is invisible at the parameter instead of being flagged at the assignment that introduces it. It is `JObject?` now, with `JObject? sdkProbe` at the caller and `JObject?[]` for the probe table in the exhaustive-verdict test. Behaviour is unchanged - the null check was always there and always ran. Measured on this branch, exactly six warning locations disappear and none is added: `CS8600` at `Program.GatewayTools.cs:736`, `:739` and `:747`, `CS8604` at `:749`, and `CS8625` at `WorkerLivenessClassifierTests.cs:127` and `:201`, taking the Release baseline from 280 to 274.
- A shared Worker that wedged mid-command was never reaped, because the log line that records the reap dereferenced a process the shared Gateway does not own. `RunHealthCheckAsync` used to be gated on `_process != null`, which a shared Gateway never satisfies - it attaches to a broker and owns no child `Process` - so every check below it was unreachable for a shared Worker. Issue #335 changed the gate to `hasVitals || sharedAlive` and introduced the null-safe `workerLabel` (`childPid` from the broker, or `"shared"`) for the two log lines in the branches it touched. It missed the third: `worker_wedged_shutdown` still read `_process.Id`. A `NullReferenceException` is thrown from inside the interpolated string, the loop's own `catch (Exception)` swallows it, and `StopProcess(WorkerStopReason.Wedged)` on the very next line never runs - so the one condition that exists precisely to reclaim a wedged Worker is the one condition it could not reclaim. The path needs an in-flight command older than `WedgedCommandTimeoutMinutes` (15 by default) plus `WedgedSilenceSeconds` (120) of silence on the shared connection, which is what "wedged" means rather than "slow"; `_lastResponse` is advanced by the shared `LineReceived` handler too, so a healthy lane never reaches it. It emits no `worker_wedged_shutdown` line and only a generic `[Health] Error during health check loop`, which is the reason this was not visible as a reap failure. The line now uses the same `workerLabel` as its two siblings and reports `id=` rather than `pid=`, because for a shared Worker the value is the broker's `childPid`, not a process this Gateway owns. `mayRecycle` is deliberately **not** added to that branch here: the wedged path was already outside the broker-election gate, and deciding whether a shared reap needs one is a separate question from the null dereference.
- `IndexSnapshotIncrementalPublicationTests` cleaned up after itself only where each test remembered to. Its class-level `Dispose` deletes the per-test temp KB trees it accumulates in `_kbPaths`, but the class never declared `: IDisposable`, and xUnit only disposes a test-class instance through that interface - so the method was unreachable, and `xUnit1013` was reporting it as a public method on a test class that should have been a `Fact`. Every other temp-tree test class in `GxMcp.Worker.Tests` declares `: IDisposable`, and this was the only one flagged. **The leak is latent rather than active**: the eight tests that touch disk each already call `cache.DeleteOnDiskSnapshot()` in a `finally`, and the four that do not were measured to create no directory, so nothing accumulates today. What was wrong is that the safety net existed and was not wired up, so the first test added without an inline `finally` would leak silently and nothing would say so. Adding the interface activates a method that is already fully guarded (`Directory.Exists`/`File.Exists` per candidate, `catch { }` per delete), and on the current tests it is a no-op after their own cleanup. Both fixes were mutation-checked against their own diagnostic: reverting the log line restores `CS8602` at `WorkerProcess.cs:486`, and dropping `: IDisposable` restores `xUnit1013` at line 48.
- The incremental build no longer ships an assembly compiled from source it never regenerated. Cleanliness is decided solely by `EditDirtyTracker`, and the only route a write surface has into it is `WriteService.NotePerTargetWrite` - but `genexus_properties action=set`, `genexus_layout set_property` and the `genexus_structure` mutating actions commit through the SDK and return success without calling it. A target that had already been built and marked clean in the same session therefore stayed clean after the edit, and the next build took the compile-only fast path against a `.cs` that Specify+Generate had never regenerated; with `fastIncremental`, an all-clean target list short-circuits to `NoBuildNeeded` without dispatching a build at all. This is the one defect in the audit that produced a wrong artifact rather than a wrong message - there was no error surface to notice. Every one of those surfaces now marks its target after the commit and never before, so a rolled-back write is not marked. `LayoutService` is marked once, at `PersistVisualXml`, which is the single SDK commit every visual XML write passes through - so the mark also covers the report control mutations in `LayoutService.ReportControls.cs`, whose callers mutate a document and hand it over rather than committing anything themselves. Two helpers in that same file are deliberately left unmarked: `TryRestoreProcedureSource` is the compensating restore on every one of its caller's failure paths, so a mark there would make a rolled-back write dirty and force a regeneration with nothing to regenerate, and `TryFlushSourceForLayoutMutation` saves inside an enclosing transaction that either commits - already marked at the commit - or rolls back, and takes no object-name argument to mark. 14 new tests, table-driven so a new write surface added without a row fails loudly. Two guards were strengthened by their own mutation checks rather than by inspection: the consumer pin asserted the bare call `!EditDirtyTracker.IsDirty(kbPath, t)`, which occurs twice in `InProcessBuildRunner`, and stayed green when the compile-only fast path was deleted because the all-clean short-circuit still matched - it now pins the assignment unique to that site; and mark counts are read with comments blanked, so a comment naming the call cannot satisfy an assertion about the call.
  Not fixed by this change, and not regressions. `PropertyService` still does not call `ObjectService.MarkReadCacheDirty` after a property write. That is a read-cache question - the next read serving pre-write bytes - and is deliberately kept distinct from the build bookkeeping fixed here; the layout path already does both, and conflating them would have made a read-cache change ship on the back of a build fix. Separately, the same missing mark is present on write surfaces outside this change's scope: `StructureService`, `RefactorService`, `MergeToolService`, `AtomicCreateService`, `AtomicAuthoringService`, `DataViewService`, `SDTService`, `PatternApplyService`, `BatchService`, `ApiIntrospectService`, `GeneratorReferenceService`, `GxServerWriteService`, `TransactionRecordsService`, `ForgeService` and the `WriteService.PatternWrite` / `.ThemeWrite` / `.VisualWrite` partials each contain SDK commit or save calls with no `NotePerTargetWrite` anywhere in the file. They are reported rather than fixed here because each needs its own commit-site audit - some delegate to a surface that marks, and marking a delegating caller as well would be the duplicate mark this mechanism is easy to grow into.
- A keyed mutation that failed before it reached the SDK no longer poisons its own idempotency key for the life of the process. `IdempotencyCache.GetOrCompute` writes a `started` fence to the durable mutation journal, then runs the factory, and only cleared that fence on success or on `ErrorNotCacheable` - every other exception escaped with the record still `started`, which the next attempt under the same key read as "a previous process may have committed this mutation" (`operation_unknown`). For the realistic pre-dispatch failures that message is false: a full Worker pool, an unresolvable KB, a rejected argument. The caller was told a write may have committed when none had run, and the only way out was a manual journal repair. The distinction that makes this safe is dispatch position, and it is now a named predicate (`PreDispatchFailureClassifier`) rather than an inline type check: membership is `UsageException`, `KbResolutionException` and `WorkerPoolFullException`, each raised by validation, KB context, or pool capacity before a command exists. `KbResolutionException` is a *sibling* of `UsageException`, not a subtype - it derives straight from `Exception` - so it is listed explicitly; and `UsageException` here is provably gateway-side, because `GxMcp.Worker` declares its own same-named type and does not reference the Gateway assembly, so worker-side usage errors cross the pipe as JSON envelopes and cannot match. **The fence is deliberately unchanged for anything raised after dispatch**: a failure once the command has been handed to a worker keeps `started`, because "may have committed" is then the truth, and the retry still gets `operation_unknown`. `OperationCanceledException` is excluded for exactly that reason - a `RequestAborted` cancellation can arrive on either side of dispatch and is indistinguishable at this boundary, so keeping the fence is the safe direction - as are `IdempotencyConflictException` (a payload signal, not a dispatch-position signal) and `ArgumentException` (the BCL raises it during marshalling, after the write is under way). The original exception is re-thrown unchanged: this fixes journal state, not the response, and `ErrorNotCacheable` still returns its result rather than throwing. 7 new tests, 2 mutations verified red - removing the predicate made the pool-full and KB-resolution cases fail, and widening it to every exception made the post-dispatch and cancellation cases fail.
- The KB use-lease registry is no longer unbounded, and bounding it changes nothing a client can observe. `KbUseLeaseRegistry._byToken` gained an entry per lease and never lost one, so a long-lived Gateway accumulated every lease it ever issued for the life of the process - each one carrying two nested per-request dictionaries with it. Entries are now reclaimed, but only when they are terminal *and* unreachable: `SessionKbContextStore` answers whether any live session snapshot still names a token, and a terminal entry no session points at cannot be read by anything. The reachability test is what makes this safe rather than merely correct, because terminality alone is not enough - `RefreshState` runs lazily, so a lease that expired long ago and was never touched again still reads `Active` in memory, and filtering on the stored state alone would miss most of the very entries the sweep exists to reclaim, silently. Terminal is therefore `State != Active || now >= ExpiresAt`. The two published behaviours that depend on an expired entry surviving are unchanged and pinned by tests: `leaseState` still reports `"expired"` rather than `"invalid"` for an expired lease a session still holds (`AnExpiredLeaseIsStillReportedWhileItsSessionReferencesIt`, driven through `Program`'s own mapping), and `Renew` still returns `Expired` so an explicit `kb=` after expiry still recovers (`ATrimmedRegistryDoesNotBreakExplicitKbRecovery`, which also pins `Validate` still raising `KB_LEASE_EXPIRED`). The sweep runs in two phases so the session store is asked with the registry lock released - no code anywhere takes store-lock then registry-lock, and nesting them would invent an order - and each candidate is re-validated under the lock before removal. It triggers when the map crosses a high-water mark and trims back below it, not per request, so normal load pays a count check. A registry constructed without the predicate behaves exactly as before, which is why only `Program` opts in. 6 new tests, 5 mutations verified red; the `State != Active` mutation goes red on exactly the lazy-expiry test that exists to catch it.
- Capacity eviction can no longer deregister the wrong Worker and orphan its process. `EvictEntry` stopped the victim's `GxMcp.Worker.exe` and then removed the alias with the plain key overload, but the victim's own `OnWorkerExited` handler removes from `_entries` without taking `_capacityLock`, and `AcquireAsync`'s `GetOrAdd` does not take it either - so a Worker exiting between `SelectVictim()` and the removal let a concurrent acquire install a fresh entry for that alias, and the eviction dropped the *replacement's* entry instead. Its process kept running with nothing referencing it: absent from `ListOpen()`, `Snapshot()`, whoami and `IsAtCapacity()`, and unreachable by `StopAll()`, so it survived gateway exit holding KB file locks until its own idle timer fired. The removal now uses the conditional `TryRemove(KeyValuePair<string, Entry>)` overload that the two sibling sites in the same file already used for exactly this reason - it removes the entry that was selected, and leaves a concurrent replacement registered. No capacity policy changed: the victim scan, the idle-only eligibility rules, the `MaxOpenKbs` cap and the `WorkerPoolFullException` bail-out are untouched, and the bail-out's own plain `TryRemove` stays as written because it removes the alias the caller is about to occupy, not a previously selected victim. Four regressions cover it, driven through the real exit handler and a real `AcquireAsync` rather than by racing: evicting a stale entry keeps the replacement reachable, evicting the *current* entry still removes and stops it, the replacement's Worker is not stopped by the stale eviction and is still reachable by the shutdown path, and `StopAll()` stops every entry that is still registered.

- The post-write visual check no longer hands a caller-derived url or screenshot path to `cmd.exe` with metacharacter interpretation still live. `VisualVerifyService.DefaultCliRunner` carried its own `cmd.exe` command line and its own quoter - `"\"" + s.Replace("\\", "\\\\").Replace("\"", "\\\"") + "\""` - and that quoter escaped a backslash and a quote and nothing else. The url is built from the object name the caller asked to verify, and it reaches the driver's `/c` line unsanitized (`SanitizeSegment` applies to the baseline directory, not to the url), so a name carrying `%` arrived with environment-variable expansion live and `&`, `|`, `<`, `>`, `^`, `(`, `)` were not escaped at all. `PreviewService` spawns the same class of `.cmd` shim through `BrowserDriverProcess.BuildShimArguments`, which refuses an argument containing `%` or a control character and escapes the rest; the two copies had drifted, which is the whole reason that rule is meant to live in one place. The runner now composes discrete logical arguments and calls that shared builder, and its private quoter is deleted rather than left unused. **A refusal is a result, not a crash**: it becomes a `{skipped, reason: "DriverArgumentRefused", error}` envelope that names the driver and the problem, never the rejected value, and nothing is spawned - so it is distinguishable from a browser that failed to cooperate rather than being folded into `CaptureFailed`. A driver that is neither a native `.exe`/`.com` nor a `.cmd`/`.bat` shim - a `.ps1` reached through `PATHEXT` - is refused by the same shared rule instead of being handed to `cmd`, which is the refusal `PreviewService` already applies. Valid input is unchanged: the composed argument string re-tokenizes to exactly the logical arguments the builder would have received, so a Windows path no longer arrives with every backslash doubled, and the built command line is byte-identical to the one `PreviewService` builds for the same arguments - pinned by running both runners against a real `.cmd` shim and comparing what the child actually saw, not just what the builder returned. 3 new tests, with the duplication restored and the old quoter restored as separate mutations verified red. What is **not** claimed: whether a GeneXus object name can in practice carry `%`, `&` or `^` was not measured. The duplication and the drift are certain; exploitability is not asserted.
- `genexus_kb action=create` no longer puts the database password on the MSBuild child command line. The caller-supplied DB password was passed as a `/p:KBDbPassword=` property in `KbCreateHelper`'s argument string (`src/GxMcp.Gateway/KbCreateHelper.cs`), and on Windows a running process's command line is readable by any process that can query it. MSBuild also echoes its own invocation back in its error output, and the failure envelope returned that raw concatenated stdout+stderr as `output` - so a failed create handed the caller the credential back in the response body. The inbound request log was already masked for `dbPassword`; the leak was specifically the child command line and the response field. The value now travels in an MSBuild response file written next to the temp project and referenced by a quoted `"@path"` on the command line, so the command line carries only the property name; the `@` reference is quoted because MSBuild takes the whole token as the path and an unquoted reference under a temp path containing spaces is split by the child's command-line parser. The response file is written with a UTF-8 BOM because MSBuild decodes one as ANSI without it, and is deleted in the existing `finally` so a failed spawn cannot leave a file holding the password in the clear. The quoting round-trips values containing spaces, semicolons, quotes, trailing backslashes and non-ASCII text, all of which the previous inline `/p:` form did not - measured against .NET Framework MSBuild 4.8, not assumed. `output` in the failure envelope is now redacted through `LogRedaction` plus a call-site mask, and capped at 16 KB on a rune boundary with a truncation marker. That call-site mask is load-bearing rather than belt-and-braces: `LogRedaction`'s pattern requires a word boundary before `password`, which `KBDbPassword` does not have, so the generic call alone leaves `/p:KBDbPassword=...` intact in the response - found by writing the test and watching it fail. `/p:KBDbUser=` stays inline, since a user name is not a secret and an operator reading a failing invocation needs it. The argument string is not logged anywhere. 4 new tests, 3 mutations verified red.
- The last four KB-sourced XML parse sites now prohibit DTDs like the eight that already did. `knowledgebase.connection`, SDT structure blobs, WebForm part documents and module package manifests were parsed with a bare `new XmlDocument()`, which leaves DTD processing at the parser's default and the resolver live - and all four documents are machine-generated GeneXus metadata with no legitimate DTD. The hardened siblings made the pattern look exhaustive when it was not, which is the cost of the rule having lived in eight places instead of one, so the reader now has one definition (`SafeXml`, `DtdProcessing.Prohibit` + `XmlResolver = null` + a `MaxCharactersInDocument` bound twice `ModuleInstallPackage.MaxXmlCharacters`, the largest already in force in the Worker) with a path overload and a text overload, and the four sites route through it. Each site keeps its existing failure behaviour and log level, so a rejection lands in the `catch` that was already there: `null` at `Info` for the connection string, the partially-collected ids at `Debug` for the SDT blob, `"(parse threw: …)"` for the WebForm probe, and - verified, not assumed - the curated `InvalidDataException` for a module manifest rather than a raw `XmlException`. **The input is KB-sourced rather than directly request-sourced**, so this closes an inconsistency as defence in depth; it is not a demonstrated exploit path. What is certain is the drift itself. A source-shape guard now fails if a `new XmlDocument()` reappears in any of the four files, so the hardening is exhaustive rather than aspirational - but it covers a file list, not the codebase, so a fifth KB-sourced parse is only covered if it goes through `SafeXml` and is added to that list in the same change. 7 new tests, 2 mutations verified red.
- `genexus_whoami` no longer answers a question about KB selection that the next call will contradict. It carried a second, independent copy of the resolution policy, and the two copies had already drifted in two ways that mattered. Its alias map was built declared → open → known with last-write-wins, so a durable `known` record — the one that survives a worker recycle and can carry a stale path — silently overwrote the live `open` entry, while `KbResolver` prefers `open`; and in legacy mode it fell through to `single-open`/`declared-first` when the configured default did not resolve, reporting `selectionState: "valid"` for a call that raises `KB_NOT_FOUND`. The probe said "fine" and the next call failed, or quietly went somewhere else. `KbResolver` now exposes the decision as data (`KbSelectionResult`: handle, source, state, `contextRequired`, and the code and message it would have thrown) with no throwing path, and its throwing `Resolve` is expressed in terms of it — so there is one decision tree and the two callers cannot disagree by construction. `BuildWhoamiPayload` projects from it and keeps only its own formatting (the `kb` block shape, the `kbPath` scaffold fallback, `exists`/`looksValid`). An unresolvable configured default is now reported as `selectionState: "absent"` with `contextRequired: true` — nothing is selected, which is what it means, in the existing published vocabulary — and the code the next call will raise is surfaced in-band as `kb.selectionErrorCode` (`KB_NOT_FOUND` / `KB_CONTEXT_REQUIRED` / `KB_AMBIGUOUS`, null whenever a KB was selected) so the agent sees the failure it is about to get. Every `selectionSource` (`session-select`, `single-open`, `config-default`, `declared-first`, `explicit-arg`, `none`), every `selectionState` (`valid`, `absent`, `invalid`, `conflicting`), the error codes and the strict-mode `DefaultConflict` behaviour are unchanged, and `contextRequired` is only ever tightened in whoami, never loosened — a resolved session selection still needs an active lease. The `catch` that keeps this diagnostic endpoint from failing its caller is narrowed from `catch { }` and logged, because the decision itself can no longer throw. The agreement between the two callers is now asserted from both sides over every reachable state, and both divergences are pinned by regressions that go red when reintroduced.
- `genexus_apply_pattern`'s schema no longer promises a reapply inference the route refuses. The `pattern` property read "omit with `reapply=true` to infer from the existing instance" while the runtime answers `PatternRouteUnsupported` for that route - so a caller who followed the schema spent a turn discovering the refusal that the schema had just told it to rely on. Three surfaces carried it and all three now agree with the envelope the Worker actually returns. `pattern` says it is required for apply/diagnose (`MissingPatternKey`) and that only reapply can infer it, and that route is refused for non-WorkWithPlus patterns. `reapply` states the refusal with the reason the runtime gives - headless reapply does not regenerate derived objects - names `PatternRouteUnsupported`, and points at `genexus_edit part=PatternInstance`, which is the route measured to change the instance. The `examples` array no longer demonstrates the refused combination either: `{name: "Customer", reapply: true}` became `{name: "WorkWithPlusCustomer", pattern: "WorkWithPlus", reapply: true}`, the one case that is not refused. The refusal is **not** changed: `GenericReapplySupported` stays `false`, and the reason it is scoped rather than blanket is now stated - `IsSupported` returns early for WorkWithPlus, so the tool's own help and this schema describe a WorkWithPlus route that exists alongside the generic one that does not. The parameter also stays in the schema, deliberately: `CommandDispatcher` still reads it and dispatches `ReapplyPattern`, so removing it would have hidden the supported WorkWithPlus route instead of correcting the prose. 3 new tests, 3 mutations verified red - restoring the old `pattern` sentence, restoring the old `reapply` sentence, and deleting the `reapply` property; the last mutation turns two guards red, which is right because the second one reads that description. Follow-up to [#353](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/353), whose refusal is deliberate and unchanged.
- Compound and short credential key shapes were logged intact. The single shared redaction rule wrapped its key alternation in `\b` on both sides, so it demanded a word boundary *before* the key: a compound key whose prefix is a letter - `KBDbPassword=`, `DbPassword=`, `myToken=` - and the standard ADO.NET short alias `Pwd=` matched nothing and passed through unmasked, on all five logging paths that write through the helper. `Pwd` is the shape most likely to appear in a real connection string, and because the unmasked value pattern stops at a delimiter, a semicolon-delimited connection string was masked only up to its first `;` - so the tail, which is exactly where `Pwd=` lives, went to the log verbatim. The long keys now carry a narrow prefix allowance of word characters plus `.`, `[`, `]` and `-`, and `pwd` joins the key list. `pass` deliberately does **not** take that prefix and stays a separate strict-boundary alternative: folded into the prefixed group it matches `bypass=1` as `by` + `pass` and redacts an ordinary diagnostic value, which would trade a leak for a much quieter log, so the split is part of the fix and not an accident of grouping. Both assembly copies carry the change and the parity test that holds them equal still passes. This gap was not an oversight: it was found while consolidating five divergent copies of this helper, recorded in-source as a `KNOWN GAP` in the tests rather than left in a tracker, and deliberately deferred because widening the rule is a security change and not a consolidation. That deferred change is what ships here, and the two tests that pinned the leak - one asserting the leaking output, one asserting that `Pwd` was unrecognised - were inverted and renamed rather than deleted, because a name is documentation and a deleted assertion would leave the gap unguarded. `ThePatternExistsOncePerAssembly` also stops pinning the literal key list, which made it fail for the correct addition of a key; it now counts the `Pattern` declaration its name actually claims. A negative regression theory (`bypass=`, `compassion=`, `aPass=`, and prose carrying a key word with no separator) is what holds the prefix narrow: every one of its prefix cases was confirmed to go red when `pass` is moved into the prefixed group.
- Every remaining Worker write surface that commits its own objects now records the target as dirty, so a successful write can no longer leave the next build on the compile-only fast path shipping a stale artifact. The defect is only ever a *missing* mark, never a wrong one: `EditDirtyTracker.IsDirty` already treats any target with no explicit clean record as dirty, so the safe default was already correct and a missed `WriteService.NotePerTargetWrite(target)` is what pushes a target onto the fast path it should have left. **Thirteen services were affected**, closing out the earlier partial sweep: `AtomicAuthoringService`, `AtomicCreateService`, `BatchService`, `RefactorService`, `ForgeService`, `GxServerWriteService`, `MergeToolService`, `PatternApplyService`, `StructureService`, `WwpProjectionHelper` and the three `WriteService` partials (`PatternWrite`, `ThemeWrite`, `VisualWrite`) - 52 lines added, no signature changed anywhere. **How the scope was corrected is the part worth recording.** A previous pass classified four of these as "delegates to the write service" and therefore exempt, on the strength of a `_writeService.WriteObject` call site being present in each file. That is inference from a collaborator's field name, not tracing: `_writeService.WriteObject` in those files is the *fallback* taken when an object lookup returned null, and the path that actually commits in the normal case is the service's own transaction - `BatchService` wraps `BeginTransaction()`/`Commit()` around the batch, `RefactorService` wraps two of its own around the attribute and object renames, and `AtomicAuthoringService`/`AtomicCreateService` call `EnsureSave` directly. The rule this supersedes is the useful one: **trace `BeginTransaction` -> `Commit` -> the mark, every time; the presence of a write-service collaborator proves nothing about who commits.** `ApiIntrospectService` was audited and left untouched - it already reaches the mechanism through `WritePipeline.NoteWrite`, which is a literal scan cannot see, and a guard now pins that indirect route so it is not "fixed" a second time. `WwpProjectionHelper` is the one mark here that establishes a convention rather than following one, and the clearest user-visible bug in the set: every caller of the WorkWithPlus projection helper marks only the WWP instance and reports the parent separately, yet the helper unconditionally saves the parent `WebForm` - itself a build target - so a projection that regenerated it shipped a stale generated class. Both save paths are marked, including the `EnsureSave(true)` fallback, which is a real save of the same object and not a lesser one. **Four commit sites are deliberately left unmarked**, and a source-shape guard asserts they stay that way, because marking either would be a new defect: `StructureService.RestoreAuthoredTransactionParts` and `RestoreTransactionSnapshot` run on rollback paths and restore pre-mutation state, so a mark there records a *reverted* write as dirty - exactly the failure this mechanism exists to prevent - and `PatternApplyService.TryDirectAttachPatternInstance` and `TryInvokeBuildProcessUpdateParent_Legacy` are unreachable (the only other occurrences of either name in the tree are a comment promising a caller that never arrived and a string in a test), so marking them would mark objects no tool wrote. That guard is the layer a future "be thorough" edit needs: with it, adding a mark to `RestoreTransactionSnapshot` turns exactly one test red while the per-file table stays green. Marks are placed after the confirmed commit rather than at each pre-commit `EnsureSave`, so a transaction that rolls back leaves no dirty state - which is why the two `RefactorService` renames stamp the renamed object and its patched callers together after their single `Commit()` instead of at the eight `EnsureSave` calls inside them. 20 new tests; 17 mutations verified red, every one confirmed applied to the source before its run (13 whole-file reverts, the fast-path consumer replaced with `true`, and one mark planted in each exclusion class). The consumer pin is on the full assignment rather than the `!EditDirtyTracker.IsDirty(kbPath, t)` fragment, which occurs **twice** in `InProcessBuildRunner.cs` and therefore stayed green with the compile-only skip replaced by a literal `true` - verified here, not assumed. Counts are read through `RepoSource.WithoutComments` because every mark sits under a comment explaining its placement, and an unstripped count is satisfied by the comment with the call deleted. With this, the earlier declaration of partial scope for the dirty-mark sweep can be read as complete.
- A second Gateway can no longer erase a first Gateway's crash fence, so a retried idempotency key cannot replay a mutation that already committed. The durable mutation-operation journal is one fixed file per installed exe (`state\mutation-operations.json`), and the Gateway deliberately runs as several processes - `stdio-isolated` gives every AI client its own, legacy HTTP mode runs a master plus proxies - but the file's only mutual exclusion was an in-process `lock`, so two Gateways persisting the same path were last-writer-wins: process B writes a `started` fence, process A's next `Begin` serialises its own stale in-memory entry set over the whole file, and B's record is gone. A crash after B's `Begin` then read back as "never started" and the same key replayed, which is exactly the property the fence exists to hold. The journal now takes the same cross-process `.lock` lease its sibling `MutationRecoveryRegistry` already uses (`FileShare.None`, bounded retry), and reloads the durable entries *inside* that lease before deciding a result, so the snapshot can no longer be stale. Ordering is the process lease outermost and the in-process gate inside it, matching the registry, so no path can wait on a peer's lease while holding this instance's gate; the lease spans reload -> decide -> persist rather than only the read. Every path that persists (`Begin`, `Complete`, `Fail`, `Reconcile`) now goes through it, and the reload replaces the entry set instead of merging it, so a record a peer completed or abandoned is not resurrected. Acquisition failure **fails closed**: `Begin` returns the existing `JournalUnavailable`, which `IdempotencyCache` turns into the `operation_journal_unavailable` envelope telling the caller the write was not executed, rather than proceeding unlocked. The on-disk format, the entry model, and the `MaxEntries` / `MaxBytes` ceilings are unchanged, and a corrupt journal still takes the same pre-existing fail-closed path. 4 new tests, 4 mutations verified red: removing the reload-in-`Begin` turns 3 of the 4 red, and weakening the lease's `FileShare.None` to `FileShare.ReadWrite` turns the fourth. That fourth case is why the lease test holds the lock file twice: an exclusive holder blocks *any* incoming open, so on its own it cannot distinguish a real `FileShare.None` from a share mode that coordinates nothing, while a permissive holder shares all access and so can only be refused by the journal's own share mode. Two related details were found while testing rather than assumed. **Contention is not corruption, so it is not latched**: a peer holding the lease marks the instance busy, but that clears on the next successful acquisition, because a peer still holding the lease will release it and a permanent latch would refuse writes forever. And a journal this instance cannot coordinate no longer reports a record as `not_found` - `Inspect` had been reading the corrupt/latch flag alone, so a busy journal would have answered "no durable record exists" and invited a caller to authorise a fresh key over a live fence; it now reports `operation_journal_unavailable`, same as corruption. Both are mutation-checked too.

- The reapply refusal is now re-measured on a second major, and a `NullReferenceException` that this build caused is no longer reported as a licensing problem. Follow-up to [#353](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/353), on the same disposable KB and the same GeneXus 18.0.10.184260. Reapply was run against a **known pending difference** rather than a changed token: both routes to corrupt a generated object are already blocked - hand-editing the generated WebPanel's form is refused with `K2BDesignerEditUnsupported` because it would change K2BTools designer grid metadata, and deleting a generated object is refused by the SDK - so a `Character(10)` attribute was added to the Transaction instead, confirmed present there and absent from the instance and from all three derived objects, with an instance-save control in the same run proving the measurement detects regeneration when it happens. Reapply with no settings still throws `NullReferenceException` from inside `PatternEngine.ApplyPattern(PatternInstance, ApplySettings)`, so the GX17 U4 + K2BTools 13.1 finding reproduces on a different major and without that package. **The cause of the throw is this build's own**: `ReapplyPattern` passes `null` for `ApplySettings` on the assumption the SDK treats it as defaults, and that is what the SDK dereferences - so `PatternEngineApplyFailed` no longer advises verifying the pattern package and KB state for that case. It reports a machine-readable `cause: nullApplySettings`, retracts the licensing advice, and points at the one route measured to regenerate. Supplying settings (`IsFullGeneration` + `ForceSave`) removes the throw and returns `PatternApplied` - and regenerates nothing: all three derived objects byte-identical, pending difference intact. **So the route stays refused, deliberately.** The NRE is fixable on our side, and fixing it would trade a loud refusal for a silent no-op, which is why this is a diagnosis fix and not a gate fix. The refusal reason now records that it was re-measured rather than inherited, so a caller can tell a confirmed refusal from an assumed one. Full protocol, evidence and the discriminator's own limitation are in `docs/sdk-probe/workwith-regeneration-candidate.md`. 4 new tests, 6 mutations verified red. Two guard holes were closed rather than papered over: one asserted only that an identifier appeared in the source and stayed green when the branch was assigned `false`, so it now asserts the exact condition (`reapply && ex is NullReferenceException`); another matched a sentence the source splits across a string concatenation, so it asserts a phrase that survives. Both were found by the mutation check, and a third stale-binary result during this work traced to rebuilding the Worker project without refreshing the copy inside the test project's output.- Two answers to [#353](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/353) that the code was getting wrong, plus the measurement that settles one of them. The issue asked whether an existing **standard** WorkWith's derived objects can be regenerated headlessly on GX18, and accepted "no known candidate" as an answer.
