Two things came out of reading the installed SDK and then running the experiment the issue specified. **First, a candidate exists and is named**: `Artech.Packages.Patterns.PatternEngine.GenerateInstanceObjects(IPatternBuildProcess, PatternModel, PatternInstance, InstanceObjects, ApplySettings, ApplyResults)`, an `internal static` in the Artech engine, reachable by reflection the way the WorkWithPlus projection already is, with all six arguments obtainable from the installed SDK and no vendor package required. Standard WorkWith ships the same `IPatternBuildProcess` contract WorkWithPlus does - `Artech.Patterns.WorkWith.WorkWithPattern.GetBuildProcess()` is public with a parameterless constructor - so the two patterns are structurally symmetric and the asymmetry the reporter could not resolve has a shape: `UpdateParentObject` only *projects* onto the bound parent, which is the whole job for WWP-on-a-WebPanel because the generated object *is* the parent's projection, while standard WorkWith on a Transaction produces separate KBObjects and needs the driver that materialises them. **Second, and against the grain of the recorded evidence: regeneration works, and it does not go through reapply at all.** A plain `genexus_edit name=<Trn> part=PatternInstance` regenerated the derived family on this machine, on 18.0.10.184260, with no IDE, no K2BTools and no reflection - three times, in both directions, with the changed `selection/@description` landing in the generated list's `TitleText` control and both derived objects returning to their exact baseline byte lengths on the revert. The prior negative evidence was GX17 U4 + K2BTools 13.1 and concerned the two `ApplyPattern` overloads; it did not cover this environment or this trigger. `GenericReapplySupported` stays `false` - the reapply route remains refused, because nothing here tested it. What changes is that two surfaces no longer state a falsehood: the reapply rejection reason claimed "headless reapply does not regenerate the pattern's objects" as a property of the engine, when it is a property of two named overloads on one recorded combination, so it now names the overloads, the combination, and the unmeasured candidate beside the verdict (in a new `unverifiedRegenerationCandidate` field, so a client branching on `supported` is unaffected). And the `PatternInstance` write returned `generatedObjectsRegenerated: false` for every non-WorkWithPlus pattern - flatly untrue on GX18, and still true on GX17 U4 + K2BTools 13.1, which this build still supports, so the field is **removed rather than flipped** and replaced by `derivedObjectRegeneration`, which reports both environments' evidence and says plainly that the call site does not measure. Measuring it properly means re-reading the derived family inside a write that has already committed, which is a separate change and not a string edit. The mechanism behind the observed regeneration is **not** identified and is not claimed: `SetPatternApplyOnSave` is invoked only on the WorkWithPlus path, so this is not MCP-driven, and the leading hypothesis is the SDK's own instance save hook (`PatternInstance.OnBeforeSaveKBObject` / `OnAfterSaveKBObject`, and the instance root carries `updateTransaction="Apply WW Style"`) - but only the observable behaviour is established. One more measured detail worth not rediscovering: a **no-op** instance save returns `WriteNoChange` and still moves the derived objects' `versionToken`, so the trigger is saving the instance rather than changing it, and a token is not evidence that regeneration was needed. Evidence, member list and the full protocol: `docs/sdk-probe/workwith-regeneration-candidate.md`. 18 new tests, 10 mutations verified red - including restoring the falsified `false`, adding a hardcoded `true` to the same object, deleting the contradicting GX18 evidence, and re-advertising the removed field in the help. One guard had to be strengthened after a mutation exposed it: forbidding the single spelling `derivedObjectRegeneration"] = true` stayed green when a `["verdict"] = true` was added to the same literal, so the guard now pins the literal's exact key set.- A layout control written by the IDE is no longer reported as not there, and a control property the generator cannot read is no longer accepted with a success. Three defects, all on the same path, all presenting as "the property did not take" - which is why the report could not distinguish them. First, control lookup was case-sensitive: `FindControlElement` asked XLinq for `Attribute("ControlName")`, and that overload resolves case-sensitively, against a WebForm that spells its identity `controlName` as the SDK and the IDE both write it. Every lookup returned null, so `genexus_layout set_property` and `genexus_properties set` answered `ControlNotFound` for a control `get_tree` had just listed, on a plain WebPanel with no pattern instance; the `OrdinalIgnoreCase` in the surrounding comparison never got a chance, because the lookup had already failed. The read is now case-insensitive, which also lets the sibling helper on the typed property path stop enumerating `id | ControlName | controlName | InternalName | name` by hand - that duplication is what made the two paths disagree about whether a control exists - and stops the property-delta detector reading a document as a wholesale remove-and-re-add when a write merely changes an attribute's casing. Second, `GxFormat` is not a WebForm attribute at all: the SDK declares it on `HTMLATT`/`HTMLSPAN`/`HTMLSFLCOL` in its own `ControlDefinition.xml`, but the IDE persists it as a `PATTERN_ELEMENT_CUSTOM_PROPERTIES` payload, so a bare `format="HTML"` was stored, verified, and then ignored by the generator - a call that reported success and had no effect. `genexus_layout set_property` now refuses that property with `ControlPropertyNeedsCustomProperties` and a hint carrying the exact payload to write plus the numeric values (`0=Text, 1=HTML, 2=Raw HTML, 3=Text with meaningful spaces`); the refusal fires before the visual read, so the caller is told the real problem instead of a lookup failure. A dry-run preflight flags the bare attribute on `preflightWarnings` with a `fix` attached, scoped by the element's own hint table so the legacy `gxTextBlock` - which really does persist Format plainly, and was observed doing so - is not warned about, and an element already carrying the carrier (what the IDE writes) is never flagged. Third, fixing the lookup exposed two write-path defects that had been masked by it, and both are live-verified: the `Caption` branch compared case-insensitively and then wrote the hardcoded `Caption` spelling, so on a control spelling it `caption` it *added* `Caption` beside it and the SDK save failed with "J&aacute; foi adicionado um item com a mesma chave" - a message naming neither the attribute nor the control; writes now reuse the spelling already present and collapse case-only duplicates onto one attribute. And verification read a caption from the plain attribute only, while the SDK stores it as `CaptionExpression` Tokens XML on the HTMLATT-shaped controls, so a correct write was compared against the stale string, failed verification and was rolled back - discarding the caller's change for a reason invisible in the response; both homes are now consulted, tokens first. Live-verified on a plain WebPanel with no pattern instance: the refusal, an in-place `Caption` update, and the documented `PATTERN_ELEMENT_CUSTOM_PROPERTIES` workaround persisting and reading back. `Format`/`GxFormat` remain settable through that payload rather than a new typed route, because the SDK's own writer is the carrier and duplicating it would add a second representation to drift. Fixes [#360](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/360).
  Not fixed by this change, both observed on a plain WebPanel while verifying it and both pre-existing rather than introduced here. `genexus_edit_form action=add_textblock` still answers `WebFormWriteIndeterminate` and persists nothing, which is the first row of the report's route table; the typed create router behind it is already disabled in `ApplyEditableXml` because on every measured GeneXus 18 major the SDK strips the control it creates, so adding a control still means writing the form XML, not calling that action. Separately, a `genexus_layout set_property` that succeeds can be followed by a `genexus_read` serving pre-write bytes at an unchanged `versionToken`, while `genexus_layout find_controls` on the same object reports the new value - the layout path's read-back and the object read path disagree about what was persisted. The same sequence through `genexus_edit` moves the token and reads back correctly, and the read-cache dirty-mark is already called on the layout commit, so the cause is not identified here and no cache change is shipped on a guess. Neither was reproduced on a well-formed panel: the observation came from a scratch panel whose form was hand-authored, which the SDK's own pattern validator rejects for structure, so it is reported rather than diagnosed.
- `genexus_kb_diff` can no longer report "no differences" about two KBs it never actually compared. It used to detect changes with max-mtime and part count, so two different `Source.txt` files written with identical timestamps came back identical, and a KB root with no `Objects/` directory came back as a successfully compared *empty* model - indistinguishable from the honest answer. Change detection is now SHA-256 over each object part file, combined per object, so equal timestamps with different bytes is a change and equal bytes with a different timestamp is not (that lands in `metadataOnly` rather than `modified`). Identity and completeness are stated on every call: `identityAuthority: filesystem-directory-names` with `moduleQualified: false`, because the inventory is the on-disk tree and not a GUID-backed SDK identity, and `inventoryA`/`inventoryB` each carry a `state` of `complete`, `empty`, `incomplete` or `unavailable`. An absent, unreadable, partially readable or truncated inventory yields `status: Incomplete` and `complete: false`; only a proven empty `Objects/` reports an empty model. An object directory with nothing readable is listed under `unreadableObjects` rather than being invented as a difference in either direction. `mode=metadata` restores the old cheap heuristic and is labelled as not a content comparison; `maxObjects` (default 50000) bounds the enumeration and reports truncation. `status` stays `Success` for a complete comparison so existing callers keep working, and `complete` is the field to branch on. Both cases the issue reproduced are covered by failing regressions. Fixes [#354](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/354).

- Paging a large catalog by cursor no longer re-sorts the entire candidate set on every page. The resume predicate mirrors the sort tuple exactly (LastUpdate descending, then Name, then Guid ascending), which makes it a prefix of the total order - but the implementation evaluated it by sorting all N candidates and then scanning for the resume position, so any cursor request took the full-sort path no matter how small the page was: walking P pages over an M-entry catalog cost P x O(M log N). The predicate is now applied as a filter *before* selection, which lets a cursor page use the same bounded top-K heap the first page already used, and the result is identical page for page - pinned against an exhaustive sort over tie-heavy catalogs with equal timestamps, case-differing names and repeating GUIDs, and across a mutation between pages. The reported `total` remains the whole candidate set rather than the remaining tail, so `total`/`hasMore`/`nextCursor` keep their existing meaning. Fixes [#346](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/346).

- The lite-index walk no longer scans its whole accumulated entry list once per walked object. Deduplication was `list.RemoveAll(e => e.Guid == guid)` before appending, so a cold walk over N unique objects performed N(N-1)/2 GUID string comparisons - 49,995,000 at 10,000 objects and 799,980,000 at 40,000 - and could never find a duplicate, because a fresh walk has none. Replaced with a GUID-to-slot map and a tombstoned slot, compacted once after the walk: O(1) per object, with ordering and replace-in-place semantics unchanged, since the replacement is still appended last exactly as remove-then-add produced. A resume checkpoint is the one case where a GUID can legitimately occupy two positions, and those GUIDs fall back to the original removal so the outcome stays bit-identical rather than merely close. Fixes [#337](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/337).

- Inserting a stored source no longer re-sums the whole catalog to check the storage budget. `EnforceStorageBudget` added up `FileBytes` across every record on every insert, so populating a KB with S sources cost O(S²) accounting iterations while staying comfortably below the budget - quadratic work whose only purpose is to recompute a total it already knows. The service now keeps a tracked byte total: a replacement applies the delta (subtracting the previous record first, so a longer body is not double-counted), eviction subtracts, and a failed delete or a bulk catalog load marks the total untrusted so the next check rebuilds it with a single pass. The invariant is "reconcile at most once per desync", not "assume the counter is always right" - a fast counter that drifts would be worse than the scan it replaced, so the exactness is pinned against the bytes actually on disk across inserts, longer and shorter replacements, repeated identical writes and concurrent insertion. Two things this surfaced: clearing the store directory for tests left the counter describing the previous record set, and an in-process visit counter cannot see a full-catalog sum reintroduced at a different call site, so the authoritative complexity guard is a source-shape assertion on `EnforceStorageBudget` rather than a behavioural one. Fixes [#338](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/338).

- Warming a KB is no longer a one-shot privilege of whichever KB the Gateway happened to open first. The first-touch warm pass was claimed with a single static flag for the whole process, so once any KB had been warmed every later KB - and every Worker generation, including a recycled one - inherited that claim and paid the first-touch JIT/SDK cost inside the agent's turn, which is the exact cost the pass exists to move out of it. The claim is now keyed by (KB, Worker generation): each warms once, a replaced generation warms again, and eight concurrent requests for one live Worker coalesce onto a single pass instead of racing eight passes against the same process. A pass that throws releases its claim rather than disabling warming for that KB for the rest of the process, and a cold start whose probe object is not listable yet leaves the scope claimable so the index-bootstrap trigger can still warm it - which is the ordering that path exists for. The warm pass's source-search command also gains an explicit `objectName`: it previously passed the probe object's name as a text `pattern` with `maxResults=1`, and a result limit caps the reply, not the work, so a name absent from every stored source turned the warm-up into a whole-catalog no-match scan of the very cost it was paying. Fixes [#340](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/340).

- Opening a KB at the open-KB limit can no longer stop a Worker that is busy. The eviction victim scan skipped only entries with no Worker, so "least recently active" was the sole criterion and a Worker halfway through a build, write or index pass was eligible - which contradicts the documented idle-only policy, and for an isolated Worker means the process dies with the command on it. Entries now carry an in-flight command count, and the victim must have a Worker, be neither spawning, draining nor reloading, and have nothing in flight; when nothing qualifies the pool reports itself full instead of stopping work in progress. The count is taken at the single point every tool call acquires its Worker, so build, write and index traffic are all covered by one registration rather than three call sites that could drift, and the reservation shares the lock that guards the capacity decision so a command cannot be admitted to an entry between the eligibility check and the stop. The releaser is idempotent and cannot drive the count negative - a negative count reads as "not busy", which is precisely the condition that would let a busy Worker be evicted. Fixes [#333](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/333).

- The pattern diagnostic no longer recommends the route it refuses. `genexus_apply_pattern mode=diagnose` emitted an `overrideConflict` finding whose remediation said to reapply "to regenerate the existing pattern instance" from a constant, in the same response where `routeUnsupported` declared reapply unsupported - so for a pattern with reapply blocked, it recommended precisely what it had just declined. The remediation, the severity and the advertised route are now all derived from the same two capability answers, exposed as `reapplyRecommended`, `regeneratesDerivedObjects` and `remediationRoute`. WorkWithPlus is deliberately *not* folded into the generic engine's regeneration switch: it has its own supported apply-on-save route, so it keeps recommending reapply, from its own constant, rather than inheriting the generic engine's negative. The `routeUnsupported` hint and the `PatternRouteUnsupported` rejection hint also now qualify the permitted edit - reading and changing existing supported properties is available, which is not structural authoring and does not regenerate the derived objects. Fixes [#352](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/352).

- The `edit_pattern_instance` recipe and the `genexus_edit` help no longer promise capabilities the write path does not have. The recipe told the caller the persist response "includes childrenOrderedListReconciliation showing what the auto-reconciliation changed" - no such field is emitted anywhere in the production write path, and the same recipe's steps demonstrated raw `Insert_After` and group-insertion edits that the `PatternInstance` structural preflight rejects as `PatternStructureChangeUnsupported`. Both surfaces now show a supported property change, name the published typed action (`genexus_wwp add_user_action`) for structure, state that a group insert has no MCP route, and say that saving the instance does not regenerate the pattern's generated objects. The help's own restriction ("limited to existing property changes") was already correct and is kept; the examples contradicted it. Fixes [#352](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/352).


- A `PatternInstance` patch preview can no longer approve a change the write path refuses. `mode=patch` returned its `Applied` dry-run envelope before reaching the structural preflight, so adding a `filterAttribute` previewed green and then failed red through `mode=full` on the same bytes - one logical edit, two different answers. The patch route now runs the same `PatternXmlEditPlan` over the same bytes the write would send, and a passing preview says which preflight ran instead of leaving the caller to guess whether the check was only textual. Both routes also normalize line endings where the plan is built: the patch route was handing it `ToSdkLineEndings(content)` while a full write handed it the raw decoded content, so one document could be compared in two encodings and report a difference the SDK would never see. `PatternVirtual` keeps its structural authoring contract, and the grid/WebComponent exceptions the plan already encodes are unchanged. Fixes [#350](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/350).

- A `notifications/resources/updated` frame now invalidates what it says changed. The handler read the KB's current semantic-cache revision and broadcast it without ever advancing it, so a change made by another client or in the IDE left the KB's cached reads eligible and told subscribers a revision that had not moved. It now invalidates the affected KB scope and the derived entries naming the object, then broadcasts the revision it produced. Three things follow that did not before: an entry cached before the change is unusable afterwards, a subscriber comparing revisions learns something moved, and the existing in-flight fill guard - which refuses to cache a read whose captured revision has moved - finally has something that moves it. Scope stays per-KB rather than a global wipe, which would also discard unrelated KBs; a change that cannot be attributed to a KB falls back to the whole store, because it is not safe to attribute to a narrower one. Fixes [#336](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/336).

- `genexus_wwp action=list` no longer answers with a .NET exception. The reported symptom was a `NullReferenceException` reaching the caller as a localized "Object reference not set to an instance of an object" with nothing to correct, while the same call succeeded when `name` was the pattern host. The mechanism was `PatternAnalysisService.FindPatternPart`, which walked `instanceObj.Parts` and dereferenced `p.Name` unguarded - a WebPanel carries many parts and the SDK does not promise a non-null name on all of them, so the throw came from inside a predicate that only meant to compare strings. The comparison is now null-safe in all three forms, a null part element is skipped, and a part collection that cannot be enumerated reads as "absent" instead of crashing; every caller already turned null into a typed error, so no matching behaviour changed. Separately, the `WwpActionService` catch-all stopped repeating `ex.Message` verbatim - it now names the failing stage and the exception type, and tells the caller to retry against the instance object directly. A raw framework message as an API answer is what made the report look unreproducible. Fixes [#332](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/332).


- The STA scheduler no longer accepts unbounded work, and no longer lets a read starve everything else. Issue [#341](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/341): the per-client queues had no admission bound, so a burst could retain an arbitrary number of requests and their payloads with nothing to stop it, and the wait was strict-priority, so a sustained P0 stream could defer already-accepted P1/P2 work forever - leaving a caller that was told "accepted" with no signal that would let it recover. Admission is now bounded by both count and retained payload bytes, decided inside the same critical section as the enqueue so a command reported as refused can never execute later; the refusal reaches the caller as a `WorkerQueueSaturated` error, reusing the existing `WorkerBusy` wire shape, because a silently dropped command leaves the client waiting for a response that will never arrive. Accounting is released as items leave the queue - on dequeue and on timeout - and clamped at zero, because counters that only grow turn a bounded queue into a permanent self-inflicted denial of service, and a negative count reads as "not full" forever. Service stays priority-first for interactive latency, with one change: a lower-priority item that has waited past the aging window outranks a freshly-arrived interactive one. A default (unset) enqueue timestamp is not read as a long wait - that is missing data, not evidence.
- `genexus_inspect` no longer discards the result of its section timeout. Issue [#334](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/334): the parallel metadata sections were awaited with `Task.WaitAll(tasks, 5s)` and the returned bool was dropped, so a section that missed the budget kept running - still reading live SDK objects off the owner STA, and still writing into the response object the method had already returned and could cache. The result is now inspected, each section carries a name so a late one is reportable, and an inspection with unfinished sections says so in `sectionsComplete` / `incompleteSections`. That last part is the point the discarded result was hiding: a missing `callers` key was indistinguishable from "this object has no callers", which is a wrong answer rather than a missing one.

- The trigram source index now reclaims postings instead of only accumulating them. Issue [#344](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/344): `Put` added the new content's memberships and removed nothing, so replacing a source whose trigrams barely overlapped left the old trigrams still posting that record key - a search for text the source no longer contains kept returning it as a candidate, and the residue grew with every source churn. Each record's current trigram set is now tracked, the previous set is subtracted on replacement, and eviction removes postings too (it previously dropped the catalog record and the file while the index kept the entry reachable). A posting set left empty is dropped from the index rather than retained. Two paths that were easy to miss: a record loaded from the catalog on startup had no tracked set, so its first replacement had nothing to subtract and its original postings survived every subsequent change forever - the catalog rebuild now records them; and both sites that reset the store forget the map, or the next `Put` into a fresh store would subtract postings belonging to the store just left. Removal is per record and under the same per-set lock the writer uses, so a concurrent write is not undone. Measured: replacing a record's source takes the index from 10 postings to 9, with none of the superseded trigrams left behind.
- Log diagnostics no longer depend on the lane they are diagnosing, no longer retain the whole log, and health no longer equates a live transport with a healthy SDK. Three separate defects, all in the same diagnostic surface, which is why they surfaced together. **`ReadLogs` is dispatched off the SDK lane.** It reads a log file and returns, touching no `KBObject`, no COM object and no SDK type, yet it ran through the very STA lane that might be blocked - so the one request that could explain a blockage waited behind the blockage. It is now whitelisted for the parallel dispatch path, with the audit that licenses it spelled out in the source: anything that later makes `ReadLogs` consult the KB must remove that entry in the same change, since a stale whitelist there is a COM access violation rather than a slowdown. **A tail read retains the requested window, not the file.** `ReadLogs` opened the log, read every line into a `List`, filtered that list and only then took the last N - so asking for the last ten lines of a large log allocated the whole log, on the diagnostic route an agent reaches for precisely when something has gone wrong. The new `LogTailReader` walks the file backwards in fixed blocks, counts what it does not retain, and returns `retainedLines` / `truncatedFromStart` so a caller asking "since yesterday" can tell it received a tail rather than a complete answer. `totalLines` keeps meaning what it always meant, and a `since=crash` search that finds nothing inside the window now says the marker may be older than the window instead of asserting the worker never crashed. **Health distinguishes transport liveness from SDK progress.** `genexus_connection_recover` probed `Ping`, called the result "responsive" and reported an unqualified `Healthy` - but `Ping` answers on the transport/MTA path, so a Worker with a deadlocked SDK lane replied exactly like a healthy one. It now runs a second, independent, SDK-serviced probe and reports `transport` and `sdk` separately. The three busy states are distinguished rather than collapsed: `busy-progressing` is legitimate long work and is explicitly never a recovery target, because recycling a Worker for a real build costs the in-flight work; only `busy-stalled` justifies recovery, and a lane that has not yet been observed to move is reported `busy-unproven` and left alone, since one sample cannot tell a healthy three-second call from a deadlocked one. The progress signal is a real one - `ProgressEmitter`, the single point every progress notification passes through, records it - and it is reset when a command starts, so a new command cannot inherit the previous one's liveness. Fixes [#342](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/342).
- Detailed caller-site scanning is now bounded, resumable, and honest about being either. Issue #343: `FindCallerSites` walked every recorded caller, read Source/Events/Rules in full and parsed all of it, with no page boundary, no work budget and no cancellation point - so a high-fan-in target (a shared helper called by thousands of objects) could monopolise the single SDK lane past a client deadline and leave nothing resumable behind. Four independent budgets now bound the scan, because any one alone is insufficient: counting callers ignores source size, counting bytes ignores elapsed time, counting results ignores a target with many callers that simply do not call it, and the caller-visible failure is a deadline rather than an internal counter. The response reports `complete`, `callersTotal`, `callersScanned`, `sourceBytesRead`, `results`, `elapsedMs`, `stopReason` and a `nextCursor`, and a partial scan is answered as `CallerSitesPartial` rather than `CallerSitesFound`. Three properties matter more than the bounds themselves. The resume offset advances only past a caller that was *fully* scanned - advancing past a partially scanned one skips its remaining parts on the next page, a silent hole in an answer that looks complete. A source too large for the byte budget is abandoned rather than truncated, because half a source silently omits call sites and that is worse than stopping. And a truncated scan that found nothing never reaches the guarded-zero cross-check, so a budget stop cannot be reported as a verified zero - the whole point of that machinery is to stop an unconfirmed zero from being read as "safe to delete". Callers are also de-duplicated before paging, so the same site cannot appear twice or be split across two responses. Fixes [#343](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/343).
- A valid oversized shared-transport response no longer takes down every other client. Issue #347: the shared lane has a 4 MiB UTF-8 frame bound, and when a child emitted a line exceeding it the broker broadcast an error and cancelled the host. One client's legitimately large `read`, batch or persisted-content result therefore affected every attachment instead of returning a bounded per-request outcome. The distinction the fix makes is not about size. A large frame that *parses* is a valid response that does not fit the transport, and it belongs to exactly one requester: the broker now parses it, recovers its JSON-RPC id, and sends that attachment a compact `WorkerResponseTooLarge` error carrying the real byte count, then keeps serving everyone else. The refusal is explicitly `complete:false, truncated:false`, because the alternative failure is a caller reading a refused-then-truncated payload as the whole result, and it tells the caller not to treat it as an empty one. Fail-closed handling is deliberately unchanged where it belongs: a frame that does not parse, or that is beyond a new hard ceiling of four times the frame bound, still stops the host - the oversized path must not become a way to smuggle unparseable data past the check, which is why that ceiling exists rather than an unbounded parse. An oversized frame with no request id is dropped with a log rather than broadcast, since guessing an owner would deliver one client's error into another client's stream; an oversized response whose route has already completed is logged as unrouted for the same reason. The Gateway side applies the same rule on receipt, where throwing had signalled a disconnect, while staying strict on frames it is asked to *send*. Fixes [#347](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/347).

  Not included, and worth being explicit about: the issue also asks for output budgeting *before* child transport serialization - paging large reads/batches or offering a KB-scoped persisted artifact so a legitimate large result can be returned in full. That is not done here. This change makes an oversized result fail correctly and locally instead of taking the broker down; it does not make it succeed.
- Source-store coverage validation no longer re-reads a body it has already validated. Issue #339: search obtained coverage by validating stored parts across its whole entry set before the main scan, and that validation decompressed the body and checked its hash - then discarded the content it had just read. So a narrow query, or a warm reopen, paid a full read of every unrelated source while holding a boolean. A freshness certification now records that a body was read and validated against this exact file identity and catalog hash, and the coverage probe answers from it. The catalog summary is still only an index and the integrity checks are still performed - this is not "trust the metadata". **The trade-off is stated rather than hidden:** certifying from file metadata alone would not be safe, because a body corrupted in place with identical length and timestamp agrees with every recorded field and would be reported as certified forever. That is why the certification is also bounded in age: the same body is re-read and re-hashed every 30 seconds, so a metadata-only agreement can never be older than that window, while a change of length or timestamp invalidates immediately at any age. A body damaged in place with size and timestamp preserved is still certified until the window expires - pinned as a limitation rather than claimed as a strength, and the reason the window is mandatory rather than an optimisation. Only successes are certified, because caching the failures too would make a transient read error look like a permanent "not stored" and silently shrink coverage. Every write path invalidates explicitly - put, eviction, catalog rebuild, store reset - and a certification that fails its checks is dropped rather than left behind to be re-examined on every later probe. `TryGetStoredAndFresh` deliberately keeps reading the body: its callers promote that content into the search index, so answering "fresh" from a certification while leaving the content unread would report a hit with nothing behind it. The two contracts are now separate methods. Fixes [#339](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/339).

  Not included: the issue also asks for persisted derived postings so a warm reopen does not rebuild the trigram index by reading every body. That is not done here; a reopen still reads the catalog's bodies once. This change removes the repeated per-query validation, which is the part that scaled with query count rather than with catalog size.
- Snapshot publication now reuses clean shards instead of duplicating them. Issue #345: every flush did three things proportional to the whole index, however little had changed - it bucketed every entry into a per-shard dictionary and then discarded every clean one, it copied every unchanged shard into the new generation directory, and it SHA-256'd every shard to build the manifest, including the ones it had just copied byte-for-byte. All three now scale with the change instead. Only dirty shards are bucketed, since a clean shard's content is inherited rather than serialized. Clean shards are referenced rather than copied: a hard link records the reference in the filesystem, so an unchanged shard costs no data copy and no extra disk, which is the "immutable shard reference" the design discussion asks for expressed without touching the on-disk format. And the manifest carries forward the previous generation's hash for every reused shard, because those bytes are identical by the premise of the reuse - the value is already known and does not need rediscovering by reading the file again. **The manifest schema is unchanged**, so this is an optimisation rather than a format migration and older generations and readers keep working. **Atomic certification is untouched**: the generation is still built in a temp directory, moved into place, and published by an atomic pointer replace, and the carried-forward hash falls back to hashing whenever the previous manifest did not substantiate it - a manifest that claims a hash it cannot check is worse than a slower publication. Reuse is safe because a shard is never modified in place; it is always written with `File.Create` into a fresh temp directory, so two generations share an inode but only ever read it, and the generation sweep removes one link while the survivor keeps the data alive. Any linking failure - a cross-volume move, a filesystem without links, a momentarily locked source - falls back to a copy, so the change can only ever be slower than before, never incorrect. Measured: a one-object change on a 640-entry index writes **1 of 16 shards** and inherits the other 15. Fixes [#345](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/345).

  One caveat found by measurement rather than by reading: `CreateHardLinkW` reports a transiently-locked source exactly as it reports an unsupported filesystem, so a publication could silently degrade to a full copy for no visible reason. There is now one short retry before falling back, and the link-versus-copy split is reported per publication so the degradation is visible instead of inferred.
### Internal

- The documented validation lanes now say what CI runs, and stop recommending the order that breaks the release fingerprint. `AGENTS.md` and `CODING_STANDARDS.md` each carried a copy-pasteable lane block whose **first** entry was `.\build.ps1`, while the prose a few lines beneath it said to run it **last**. An agent that pasted the block rebuilt the Worker outside the `x86` platform the solution maps it to, so the publish↔source byte identity broke, `Get-GxMcpReleaseProcessSmokeFingerprint` came back empty, and `test-release-preflight.ps1` failed for a reason unrelated to the change under test — the exact failure `CODING_STANDARDS.md` explains one paragraph later, reached by following the block. The same blocks omitted the Nexus-IDE lane and `generate-operation-contract-inventory.py --check`, both of which CI runs. The first omission is the costly one: the `.vsix` that lane builds is a required release asset that `release.yml` byte-compares against the copy inside `publish.zip`, so "every documented lane is green" was quietly weaker than it read. `.\build.ps1` is now the last entry in both blocks, both gained the Nexus-IDE lane and the inventory check, and the remaining CI-only gates were reconciled in rather than dropped. What the block is **not** claimed to be: identical to CI. It is the operator's superset — CI enforces a subset of it, plus the protected `scripts/ci/sdk-validation.ps1` lane that needs the self-hosted `gx-sdk-18` runner, which is named in prose instead of listed because no local machine can run it. Asserting the two sets were the same set would have been the other easy lie, in the other direction. A new test pins the ordering and the two lanes, scoped to the fenced code block rather than to the whole file: both documents name `build.ps1` in prose at least five times, so a locator that took the first occurrence would assert against a sentence and would keep doing so as prose is edited — passing or failing for reasons unrelated to the block. The locator instead finds the single fenced block that *runs* the script and fails loudly if that stops being unambiguous. 2 tests / 4 cases; the ordering guard was mutation-checked by moving `.\build.ps1` back to the top and confirming it went red.

- The test projects' repo-file locator is one file instead of 34. Every source-shape guard in this repo needs to read a file from the working tree rather than from its build output, and each one grew its own walker - as `FindWorkerFile`, `WorkerService`, `RepoFile`, `ReadRepoFile`, `FindRepoFile`, `GatewayFile` and half a dozen other spellings - for 34 copies across the two test projects. They are now `RepoSource`, linked into both projects as a single source file, since the two target different frameworks and cannot reference each other. It is named so that reading contents is the default: all 34 copies returned a PATH, so a guard that forgot `File.ReadAllText` around one silently asserted against a filename and passed while testing nothing - which happened twice while this work was being done, to two guards that looked like coverage. `Read` returns contents, `PathOf` and `DirectoryOf` are explicit about returning locations, and the comment records why the naming matters.

### Changed

- `genexus_read` no longer pays a cross-process lock on every call. The read path refreshed the shared mutation-recovery journal unconditionally, so every read took an exclusive `FileShare.None` lease, re-read and re-parsed the whole journal and enumerated its directory - and when another Gateway or any writer held that lease, waited up to two seconds for it while every other registry operation queued behind the same lock. It now consults a change signal first: the journal's `LastWriteTimeUtc` **and** its length, against the values recorded when this instance last loaded or committed it. That is a metadata read, so it neither waits for a concurrent writer nor blocks one, and an unchanged journal costs one stat instead of a lease, a parse and a glob. The pair is the point and neither half is decorative: the journal is committed by renaming a candidate file over it, so length is what a fence serialising to the same size would hide, while the timestamp is what two journals holding the same number of entries would be indistinguishable by; each half has a test the other cannot pass. The one commit the pair can still miss is a same-tick, same-length one, and rather than ship a check that can miss a write silently, the content is re-read at least every 250 ms, so a miss is bounded to one interval instead of lasting until the next unrelated commit. Nothing the read depended on is skipped: a fence another Gateway committed is still observed, and when the lease is genuinely contended the read gives up after 100 ms - a bound well below the write gate's two seconds - **toward the authoritative direction**, reporting the journal busy so that `isLiveTool |= !IsHealthy` sends the read to the Worker instead of letting the cache answer a state it cannot vouch for. The write gate keeps its unconditional refresh, deliberately and commented as such on both call sites, because a write refused while a peer was mid-commit is a false block; the fence confirmation a read triggers also keeps the full budget, since a confirmation rewrites the journal. No envelope, no `requireAuthoritativeRead` semantics and no response shape changed. 4 new tests, 2 mutations verified red: always taking the lease fails the unchanged-journal test, and never reloading fails the changed-journal test.

- The pattern-shadow advisory now says the same thing wherever it appears. `genexus_patch` and `genexus_write` both raised an `EditingWebFormUnderPattern` warning when a visual part is hand-edited on an object a WorkWithPlus PatternInstance covers, but the two copies had drifted into different sentences for the same advice: one told the agent to edit `part=PatternInstance` on the object, the other named the `genexus_edit` call that does it. A client cannot tell them apart, because the code and severity are identical. Both now produce one advisory from `PatternAnalysisService.BuildPatternShadowWarning`, phrased as the more actionable of the two, which is what `genexus_write` already emitted. The probe itself is best-effort - it runs while advising on a write, and a probe that threw would fail the write it only exists to warn about - so that behaviour is unchanged, as is each caller's own object resolution.

### Internal

- Two more byte-identical pairs, both pure and dependency-free. `SafeTypeName` named an SDK type for a diagnostic and existed as a private copy on both `SdkProbeService` and `SdkSurfaceProbe`; both use it to describe what an SDK member looked like when it could not be reached, so a divergence would make two reports of one object disagree about the type it was looking at. It is now `SafeTypeName.Describe`, and its safety is the point - it runs inside the failure path, often while reporting that reflection over the SDK already failed, so null, an unresolvable generic definition, or a throwing argument list all render as `?`. The attribute-declaration rendering rules were written out identically in the Table and Transaction parsers and are now `AttributeDeclaration.Render`. This is a round-trip format - rendered here, parsed back by the same two parsers - so a rule that drifted between them would emit an attribute the other reads differently, surfacing as an object that round-trips to something else rather than as an error; the detail order, the "drop a description that merely repeats the name" rule, and the fact that detail never leaks into the declaration head are all pinned. The two callers keep their own indentation, which genuinely differs, which is why the helper returns the two halves rather than a whole line.

### Internal

- The schema source of truth is located once instead of twice. `tool_definitions.json` is what defines the tool schemas, `GatewayArgsValidator` validates incoming arguments against it, and `ToolIdentity` decides which tool names are canonical - and each of those two read it through its own copy-pasted private `LocateToolDefinitions`, code-identical, with `ToolIdentity`'s comment saying outright that it mirrored the other. They now share `ToolDefinitionsLocator.Locate`, so the validator that rejects a malformed argument and the identity check cannot drift onto different files. Writing the tests for it exposed that the upward walk - the part that was duplicated, and the only part that does anything outside the deployed layout - had no coverage at all: the test project copies the JSON beside its own binary, so the first candidate always wins and the walk never runs. The search root is therefore a parameter (`LocateFrom`) so the walk is testable against a synthetic tree, covering both project layouts it looks for, beside-the-assembly precedence over a stale copy in the source tree, and the eight-level cap.

- `McpRouter` loads the same file by a third strategy - beside the executing assembly only, with no upward walk - and is deliberately left as it is, because pointing it at the shared locator would change where it finds the file in layouts where it currently reports none. That is now recorded at the call site and asserted by a test, because as it stands a dev or test run with the JSON only under the project serves tools from an empty definition set while argument validation and tool identity both still work.

### Internal

- The child-process launch settings are written once instead of four times. `BlameService`, `GithubService`, `PrDescriptionService` and `TimeTravelService` each built their own `ProcessStartInfo` with the same capture configuration, and every setting in it was added because leaving it out broke something: `UseShellExecute = false` keeps the argument string off `cmd.exe`, where the quoting `Argv` got right would be undone before `git` saw it; redirecting and closing stdin stops a child blocking on the Gateway's MCP stdio pipe; draining both streams through async handlers avoids a deadlock once one pipe buffer fills; and the second `WaitForExit()` is what flushes them. They are now `ProcessLauncher`, with the timeout branch - the one a normal run never takes and therefore the one a copy silently loses - covered for the first time by a test that confirms the child is killed rather than merely abandoned. Each caller keeps its own executable, arguments, environment, encoding choice and outcome mapping, because those genuinely differ: `TimeTravelService` returns -1 with a message where `PrDescriptionService` throws, and `BlameService` has always read git's output in the console codepage. Those differences are now asserted.

### Fixed

- The command-line quoting primitive now has a home of its own and, for the first time, tests. `ArgvQuote` lived inside `GithubService` and was called from five services - `BlameService`, `CrossBrowserService`, `GeneratedDiffService`, `TimeTravelService` and `GithubService` itself - because a general primitive had no home and sat in whichever service needed it first. It is now `Argv.Quote` in `Helpers/Argv.cs`, alongside `Argv.Join`, which replaces four copies of the same space-join-quoted-arguments loop. The move is byte-identical in behaviour and the emitted bytes are pinned by a test so it can be reviewed as a relocation.

- Writing those tests found a defect they were not written to find, and it is the most serious thing recorded in this changelog. Checked against `CommandLineToArgvW` - the parser the CRT inside `git` and `gh` actually runs - the quoting does not round-trip: `a b\` comes back as `a b\\`, and an argument containing a double quote is split, so `a\"b` arrives as two arguments and `a b\"c d` as three. A caller-supplied commit message or branch name containing a `"` therefore becomes extra `git` arguments. This is pre-existing, present identically in all five callers, and **not fixed here**: choosing a correct encoding changes what command lines the server builds and needs its own verification, which is not part of relocating the primitive. The behaviour is pinned by `ArgvQuotingTests` so it is recorded rather than assumed, and the defect is the top item on the pending list.

### Fixed

- Credential redaction is defined once per assembly instead of five times, which also closes a masking gap the duplication had already opened. `LogValue` existed in `KbImportHelper`, `MacroSuggestionService` and `Program.Http` (Gateway) and in `PreviewService` (Worker); `SharedWorkerHost.RedactDiagnostic` was a fifth. Four were byte-identical. The fifth was not: it had already added `connectionstring` to the key list, so on the other four paths a `connectionstring=hunter2` value - and a connection string's `Server=`/`db.internal` target - reached the log unmasked. The Gateway and Worker now each hold one `LogRedaction` helper, the two pattern literals are compared by a test so the copies cannot drift apart again, and dropping `connectionstring` turns six of the new tests red. The masking marker is `<redacted>` throughout, which is what every existing assertion expected; nothing asserted on `SharedWorkerHost`'s `[REDACTED]`.

### Fixed

- The mirror-root containment guard is defined once instead of twice, and is now tested at all. `TryGetRootFile` and its `DeleteFile` companion existed byte-identical in `SdkTextTreeService.Support` and `TextMirrorService.Reconciliation`; they are now `MirrorRootFile.TryResolve`/`TryDelete`, reached from eight call sites across both services. Both services orphan-clean a text mirror and then delete the files a manifest names, so the guard decides what may be deleted - duplicated, it is a traversal hole waiting on whichever copy somebody edits next. It had no tests whatsoever, so nothing would have caught that. The new coverage includes the cases the old pair never exercised: an absolute path (`Path.Combine` discards the root entirely when the second argument is rooted), four shapes of `..` traversal, a `..` that resolves back *inside* the root and must still be accepted, a root that is a string prefix of a sibling directory, and a malformed root that must not throw.

### Internal

- Every layout failure offered the same recovery step, written out 36 times. `genexus_layout action=get_tree name=target` was spelled out at each call site across `LayoutService`'s five partials, differing only in the prose explaining what the caller was checking. The prose is per-call-site and stays there; the instruction is now `LayoutGetTreeStep`, alongside the existing `VisualObjectNotFound`/`ReportPartNotFound` builders. A step that drifted to `inspect_surface`, or lost its `name`, would have sent the caller somewhere the other 35 do not, and the divergence would have read as deliberate. The neighbouring `inspect_surface` step is a genuinely different instruction and was left alone - 11 of them survive untouched. Changing the helper's action turns the new tests red, and re-writing out one migrated site turns them red too.

### Internal

- The search-rank benchmark's port of production code is now half-enforced and half-documented, after the port was found to have drifted. `SearchRankParallelismBenchmark` calibrates `SearchService.ParallelScanThreshold` - the candidate-set size at which PLINQ starts winning - so its value depends entirely on its per-item work being the production work. It cannot call production (the Worker is net48 against the GeneXus SDK, the benchmark project is net10.0), so two scoring functions are hand-ported, and nothing enforced the port. `CosineSimilarity` is still an exact port and now is held to it by a test that strips comments and whitespace and compares from the signature, so a reformat does not fail it but a changed chunk size or a dropped remainder loop does; changing the production kernel alone turns that test red. `SemanticScore` has genuinely fallen behind: it lacks production's `nameLen >= termLen` / `descLen >= termLen` length guards, inlines `ContainsIgnoreCase` as `List.Contains(term, comparer)`, and omits the Table branch that scores an attribute-member match 5000 instead of 400. A deliberately-adapted port cannot be pinned by an equality test, so the drift is recorded on the method itself and a test keeps that note from being quietly deleted; the benchmark header no longer claims the port is verbatim or that the per-item work mirrors production faithfully.

### Internal

- The `WwpActionService` read-resolve-version-check prologue is repeated in five files on purpose, and now says so in the code. All six locals it declares are read further down every one of those bodies, so the shared part is a declaration block rather than a computation: single-sourcing it means either six out-parameters at each call site or a result object renamed across roughly forty mutation lines in five rollback-sensitive files. Both trade inline clarity for a wider signature without removing any real duplication, and the per-target lock has to stay visible in the caller because it wraps the whole read-resolve-compare-mutate sequence. The comment sits at one of the five sites so this is not re-litigated.

- The redaction helper's known limits are recorded as tests rather than left implicit. The unmasked value pattern stops at a delimiter, so a semicolon-delimited connection string is masked only up to its first `;` and `connectionstring=Server=db;Pwd=hunter2` still logs its tail; `Pwd`, a standard ADO.NET alias for `Password`, is not a key the pattern recognises on its own. Both limits are present in all five copies and are unchanged by the consolidation - widening the pattern is a change to the logging rule rather than a consolidation of it, so `LogRedactionSingleSourceTests` pins the current behaviour instead of quietly widening it.

- Two more copies of the same logic, consolidated. Reading a property's current value and reading the declared type used to coerce a new value into each carried their own copy of the same case-insensitive lookup over an SDK property bag; they are now `PropertyService.ResolvePropertyEntry`. They are not independent lookups: a write reads the existing value with one and coerces against the type the other found, for the same property name, so if the two resolved different entries the property would be read from one and written as the type of another - a silently mis-set value rather than an error. `ApiIntrospectService`'s two route-plan builders - prefix-driven cloning and explicit source/target pairs - carried byte-identical copies of both the candidate classification and the edit application; they are now `ClassifyRouteCandidate` and `ApplyRouteChanges`. The only pre-existing replacement test had a single replacement, where applying splices in the wrong order gives the same answer, so the descending-offset invariant was untested: replacements are now pinned by tests with two targets whose length deltas run in opposite directions, so a wrongly-ordered splice shifts the second offset and lands between tokens. Reordering them turns four tests red, and dropping the verb+path conflict sub-lookup turns another red. The two builders deliberately still differ in one place - the prefix-driven path resets `CandidateSource` on conflict so a conflicting plan can never be written back, the explicit path reports the caller's source instead - and that difference is now pinned by a test so it is not tidied away.

- The two operation-response projections in the Gateway and the two whole-object renames in the Worker now share one implementation each. `OperationTracker.BuildOperationStatus` and `BuildOperationResult` each wrote out the same unknown-operation envelope and the same twelve identity and lifecycle fields, then the same error projection; they differ only in the two fields result layers on top. A caller that long-polls status and then fetches the result has to see the same operation, the same timestamps and the same error for both, or a poll that says "still Running" disagrees with the result it is polling for - so the parity is now pinned by tests rather than by two copies staying equal. The shared projection is deliberately a projection and not an accessor: an `OperationRecord` is mutable and written by the polling and completion paths, so every field read has to stay inside each caller's `lock (record.SyncRoot)`, and a test asserts the call sits inside the lock. `RefactorService.RenameAttribute` and `RenameObject` resolve different things afterwards - an Attribute versus a filtered object - but opened with identical guards, and the order is the contract: a missing name is a caller error needing no KB, so reporting it as `KbNotOpen` would send the caller to open a Knowledge Base it does not need. That preamble is now `RenamePreamble`, and a test asserts the argument guard precedes the KB guard. Projecting the record outside the lock, and swapping the two rename guards, each turn the new tests red.

- The unreachable-code finder is now trustworthy, and five more unreachable private members are removed with it. `scripts/find-unreachable.ps1` was added during the previous change and reported 4434 members, including obviously-used ones like `private CommandDispatcher()`: its declaration regex had a greedy type group that swallowed the member name. Two fixes followed. The parser is now token-based rather than regex-over-the-type, and it has a `-SelfTest` switch that runs eight declaration shapes (constructor with and without a base call, inline-initialised field, const, expression-bodied method, auto-property, plain method) and fails the run if any misclassifies. It now also excludes the two shapes that are structurally always unreferenced - a constructor, which is reached through `new Type(...)` and whose declared name is the class name, and a field with an inline initialiser, where the initialiser *is* the use. That took the candidate list from 54 to 14. One signal was then removed rather than repaired: a "name occurs only inside a string literal" pass, meant to catch dead reflection hooks. Its quote-stripping mis-fired on ordinary call sites and reported six live methods as dead - `Sha1Bytes` has three real call sites, `BuildControlValues`, `GetSampleValueForType`, `GetDefaultValueForType`, `WriteShardManifest` and `DescribeActiveDataStore` all have call sites too. A check that produces false positives is worse than no check, so only the reliable "no reference anywhere" signal remains. The five members removed are all superseded rather than designed: `TextPersistenceVerifier.IsSourceOrRules` (an alias of `IsCodeOrTextPart`, which is what the two live call sites use), `PatternApplyService.EnsureProbedEngine` (a lazy-probe trigger the engine adapter does by itself), `AtomicAuthoringService.EnsureSuccess` (orphaned when its already-dead caller was removed), `VisualizerService.ExtractNameAfterColon` (the live paths split on `:` inline), and `PatchService.TryGetCachedSource` (the patch source cache is written and invalidated at six sites but nothing reads it, which the field's comment now records).

- Five more unreachable private members are reported rather than removed, because each backs a capability rather than a leftover. `PatchService.VerifyPersistedSource` and `AttachPersistedSnippet` are the post-write verification and its mismatch-diagnostics slice for `genexus_patch`; `StructureService.VerifyStructurePersisted` is the equivalent for `genexus_structure`; `PatternApplyService.TryInvokeBuildProcessUpdateParent_Legacy` is the documented last-resort WorkWithPlus update path; and `PatternAnalysisService.ResolveWWPInstanceFresh` resolves a parent to its WorkWithPlus instance. Each is unreachable today, so the behaviour behind it is not happening - but deleting an unused verifier or an unwired fallback removes a capability rather than dead weight, and wiring it up is a functional change. They are left in place and recorded here. A sixth item is a write-only cache: `PatchService._sourceCache` is populated and invalidated but never read, now that its only reader is gone.

- Eight unreachable private members are removed, and one advertised capability is now reported rather than deleted. A reachability sweep over the Worker and Gateway (`scripts/find-unreachable.ps1`) found private members with no reference anywhere in `src/`. C# does not warn on an unused private method, and the duplication scan does not see them either, which is how they survive every other check. The eight removed were all clearly superseded rather than designed: `BuildService.GetStatusValue` (its one-line body is inlined in the live status path), `IndexCacheService.GetJsonHash` (an MD5 helper left behind when the index moved to `GetFileSha256`), `LayoutService.TryParseColorToken` (a thin alias to the canonical `ColorHelper.TryParseColor`) and `LayoutService.NormalizeTextPreview` (the compaction is no longer part of any live path), `AtomicAuthoringService.ApplyAndRecord` (a leftover wrapper whose siblings are all still called), `CodeMetricsExtractor.RxEndFor` (the only one of seven sibling regexes never referenced - the `for each` count is computed, the matching `endfor` count never was), `ListService.BuildItem` (a wrapper for hoisting the legacy-profile env lookup that every call site now does itself), and `IoRouter.ConvertAssetToolCall` (the payload builder for a versioning-umbrella dispatcher that no longer exists). `ListService.BuildItem` carried the only written record of why the legacy-profile lookup must be hoisted per page rather than per row, so that note moved onto `BuildItemInternal` instead of being deleted with it. The sweep also flagged four methods in `ModuleService` - `PreviewInstall`, `PreviewInstallByName`, `PackageResultDetails` and `PackageValidationError` - which are unreachable but back a capability the tool schema advertises: `genexus_module`'s description states that `install`/`install_builtin`/`update` accept `dryRun=true` for a read-only preview. That is an advertised-but-unwired path, not dead code, so it is left in place and reported rather than removed.

- Two unreachable print-command synchronisation paths are removed, taking 150 lines with them. `LayoutService.SourcePersistence` carried `TryRenamePrintCommandInSource` and `TryInsertPrintCommandInSource` alongside the `*InMemory` versions that actually run. Neither had a caller anywhere in the Worker, no test referenced them, and nothing reached them by reflection - they were added in the same commit as the in-memory versions and never wired up. The C# compiler does not warn on an unused private method, so they sat there through every subsequent change. They were not merely redundant: each read Source as a JSON payload and wrote it back through a temp file and `ImportObjectFromText`, which is *outside* the transaction `BeginReportMutation` opens. Had they ever been called, a report print-block edit would have been persisted outside the snapshot that the rollback path restores. Removing them also removed their only caller of `TryPersistSourceText`, the helper that performed the temp-file write, so that went too. Report print-block synchronisation now goes through the `ISource` part and nothing else. The public surface is unchanged - all three methods were private - and a new test pins both halves: the in-memory path is present and assigns to `sourcePart.Source`, and the write-through path (its temp-file prefix, its `ImportObjectFromText` call, its `TryPersistSourceText` helper) is absent.

- The three print-block mutations now share one prologue. Renaming, adding and deleting a print block each wrote out the same five steps: resolve the target, load the report surface, require a report part, require an open KB, snapshot the Procedure source. The order is the contract, not the individual steps - the snapshot has to be taken after the KB is confirmed open and before anything is written, because it is what the rollback path restores. A copy that dropped or reordered a step would still compile, still look right, and would fail as a report that could not be put back the way it was. The steps are now `BeginReportMutation`, and each mutation unpacks the result under the local names its body already used so the transaction block below is untouched. The `ReportPartNotFound` and `KbNotOpened` guards this replaced were themselves single-sourced earlier; the guard test that pinned that has been updated to the new invariant - three mutations, one prologue, one envelope each - and a new test pins the prologue's own ordering, including that the snapshot sits past the `return setup;` that closes the KB guard rather than merely textually after it. Reordering the snapshot above that guard, and duplicating the report-part guard, each turn the tests red.

- The CSS lexer in the Design System source parser is now written once. `ValidateBalance` and `FindMatchingBrace` each carried their own copy of the same state machine - line comments, block comments, single- and double-quoted strings, backslash escapes - and the only thing that differed was what they did with each structural character. Two copies of a lexer is two chances for the string rules to disagree, and the failure is silent rather than loud: a brace inside a comment or a quoted selector shifts the reported nesting depth, which surfaces as a spurious "unbalanced" diagnostic, or worse, a block boundary found in the wrong place so one declaration block is parsed as two. Neither reads as a lexer bug at the call site. They now share one `ScanCode`. `StripComments` keeps its own walk and now says why: it has to re-emit comment characters as blanks to preserve offsets, where the shared lexer skips them. Thirteen new tests cover the balance check against comments, quoted braces, and escaped quotes, and confirm the checker did not become lenient about genuinely unbalanced input; dropping the escape rule - so a `\"` inside a value ends the string and the rest of the value is lexed as code - turns two of them red.

- The caller's impact walk and its dependency walk, and the two `semantic_ops` batch surfaces, now share one traversal and one loop each. `CallerGraphService` carried the same breadth-first search twice - once over `CalledBy`, once over `Called` - down to the cycle guard, the node cap, the `Truncated`/`Depth` reporting, the cancellation short-circuit and the progress emission. Both walks feed the decision of what to recompile, so a cap that drifted between them would make an impact analysis and a dependency listing disagree about the same object, and neither would look wrong. They differ only in which neighbours they expand, so that is now the only parameter. `SemanticOpsService` had the same shape one level up: the XML form and the Transaction structure DSL form each ran the batch loop verbatim, differing only in the delegate they dispatched through. That loop is the client-facing contract - a `UsageException` keeps its own code, anything else is `internal_error`, a failed op is recorded rather than thrown, `strict` stops at the first failure and `best-effort` carries on, and each result carries the request index so a client can map results back to what it sent. Written twice, that was eight places the two surfaces could start answering differently about the same request. Thirteen new tests cover the short-circuits, the abort boundary, result ordering and the mode echo on both surfaces; changing the truncated depth from the child level to the parent, and making a `UsageException` no longer abort a strict batch, each turn them red.

- The LLM-supplied path-segment allowlist is now one guard per assembly instead of three hand-rolled copies. `genexus_time_travel` (where the segment also reaches `git` as an argument), the `genexus_api` baseline name, and the Gateway's filesystem-level object import each carried their own copy of the same character class - letters, digits, `_`, `.`, `-`, with `.` and `..` rejected separately because the allowlist admits them. All three have a ceiling too, and they disagreed: 200 on two of them, 64 for baselines. That disagreement is the whole risk. A guard like this is worth exactly one copy, because a drifted allowlist is a traversal hole that opens only on the entry point nobody updated - here `..\..\x` escaping `Objects/<Type>/<Name>/` on the import path, which deletes then overwrites a directory tree. `KbImportHelper.IsSafeSegment` and `TimeTravelService.IsSafeObjectName` now delegate to `SafePathSegment.IsSafe`, and `ApiIntrospectService.IsSafeBaselineName` keeps its tighter 64-character ceiling by passing it, so no entry point became stricter or looser than it was. The two assemblies cannot share a type, so each has one and a test compares the admitted character class, the traversal rejection and the ceiling literally across both files; allowing `/` in either copy turns three of the new tests red.

- The per-object lock file key and the DSL Attribute type application are each computed in one place, and the Table and Transaction DSL parsers no longer carry their own copies. `WritePipeline` and `MultiAgentLockService` each held a byte-identical `Sanitize` that builds the filename both use to name a target's lock file. That one matters more than the duplication: two implementations of a lock-file key can disagree on a single character class, and a disagreement means two processes holding different filenames believe they hold the same object - or two names for one lock. Either way the per-target mutual exclusion stops working, and it fails as a lost update rather than as an error anyone would see. It is now `LockFileKey.Sanitize`, with the character class pinned exhaustively rather than sampled. That test also records a pre-existing property worth keeping: `char.IsLetterOrDigit` is Unicode-aware, so an object legitimately named `Ordensaché` keeps its accented characters instead of being folded, which would otherwise change the lock file for an object that already has one. Separately, `TableDslParser` and `TransactionDslParser` each applied a DSL type string to an Attribute, and the two copies were not equivalent - only the Transaction one unwrapped the occurrence through the SDK's ambiguity-resolving property lookup, because a bare `GetProperty` throws `AmbiguousMatchException` on a derived type that shadows an inherited `Attribute` property, which is exactly what `TransactionAttribute` does. The Table copy caught that exception and silently kept the occurrence itself, then wrote a type onto it. Both now call `AttributeTypeApplier.ApplyFromDslType`, so they cannot pick different Attributes out of the same SDK build, and neither parser resolves a Domain or writes a primitive itself. A mutation that added `@` to the sanitiser's safe set, and one that swapped the ambiguity-resolving lookup back to a bare `GetProperty`, each turn the new tests red.

- The Knowledge Base connection string is now parsed in one place, and the variable dimension validation no longer repeats its `out`-parameter plumbing at each entry point. `SdtModelPropagation` and `WebFormCompositionRepair` each carried a byte-identical `BuildConnectionString` that reads `knowledgebase.connection` out of the KB and decides, from its `IntegratedSecurity` element, whether a login is sent to the database server. Two copies of that branch are two chances to disagree about whether credentials go over the wire, and a caller that silently connected under different credentials is not a failure anyone would trace back here; the parse is now `KbConnectionString.Build(kbPath, logTag)`, and both callers still own the "unresolved" log because each frames the skip in terms of its own operation. The one deliberate difference: malformed KB metadata is reported at `Info` for both, so `SdtModelPropagation` gains a line it previously logged at `Debug` and `WebFormCompositionRepair` keeps the one it had - no operator loses a message. Separately, every scalar add/modify variable path opened with the same four `out` locals from `VariableDimensionSupport.TryValidate` and forwarded them straight into `McpResponse.Err`; that is now `DimensionValidationFailure`, which returns null on a valid request so the caller reads as a single guard. The batch path is a different contract - it index-prefixes the message and returns `false` into an `errorResponse` out-parameter - and is left alone. `SharedHelperTests` pins the SSPI branch across `True`/`False`/`false`/empty/absent, the null cases, and the malformed-XML case that must not throw, and asserts the pass-through of the validator envelope; inverting the SSPI comparison turns five of those tests red.

- The fail-closed mutation guards in `genexus_wwp` and `genexus_layout` are now written once instead of at every call site. `WwpActionService` re-resolves the `PatternInstance` under the per-target lock before every mutation and refuses to proceed when the re-read yields nothing usable; that refusal - same code, same message, no hint and no next steps, because the caller is mid-mutation and needs the stage rather than a detour - was written out five times across `Grid`/`Tables`/`Tabs`/`WebComponentReplacement`/`FormActions` and is now `BuildWwpInstanceNotResolvable`. It is deliberately kept separate from the other `WWPInstanceNotFound`, `BuildWwpInstanceNotFound`, which fires when the object resolved but is not a WorkWithPlus instance and has to route the caller to a different tool; a test asserts the two stay distinct so merging them cannot delete that routing. In `LayoutService`, the two guards every print-block mutation (rename/add/delete) passes through are now `ReportPartNotFound` and `ReportLayoutKbNotOpened`; the latter is a curated transient code whose `retryAfterMs` is part of the published contract, and the three report sites and the source-persistence site share the envelope while each passes its own hint. The adjacent `StaleObject` checks were left alone: `add_grid_attribute` requires `baseVersion` and validates it up front, while tables/tabs/replacement read it inside the lock and skip the check when it is absent, so unifying them would either weaken a concurrency guarantee or make a version mandatory where it is currently optional. `FailClosedGuardTests` pins the produced envelopes, asserts the two `WWPInstanceNotFound` shapes stay distinct, and guards each partial and each report mutation from drifting back; dropping `retryAfterMs`, adding a next step, and re-hand-rolling one call site each turn it red (the first also trips the pre-existing `NextStepsCurationGuardTests`).

- The three post-save variable-reference read-backs now emit one `VariableTypeNotPersisted` envelope instead of three, and the console-probe plumbing that `PreviewService` and `VisualVerifyService` each carried a copy of is a single `ConsoleProbe` helper. `WriteService.Variables.cs` wrote the Domain, Attribute and SDT/Business-Component read-backs out in full three times - identical `code`, identical `nextSteps` (`genexus_read` on the `Variables` part) and identical `extra` - with only the message and hint naming the reference kind that was lost. The pre-save "could not be represented" envelope at the `VarBuildResult` check is a different contract (no `nextSteps`, a `typeName`/`details` extra, and a message that says the variable was never created) and is deliberately left separate. Separately, `PreviewService.DefaultCliRunner.Which` and `VisualVerifyService.DefaultCliRunner.Which` were byte-identical 25-line methods, and a third copy of the same "run it, take the first non-blank stdout line" shape sat in the `npm prefix -g` probe; the runner setup, the 5 s wait and the first-non-blank-line rule are now `ConsoleProbe.FirstOutputLine` / `ConsoleProbe.Which`, which deliberately does not swallow exceptions so a timeout still surfaces to each caller's own catch. The two `Run` implementations are left alone: `PreviewService`'s has hardened argument escaping and a process-tree kill that `VisualVerifyService`'s lacks, and unifying them would be a behaviour change, not a simplification. `SharedSeamTests` pins the envelope, pins the three probe rules (first non-blank line, non-zero exit yields null even when it printed, `where` or null), and guards the read-backs from drifting back; a flipped exit-code gate and a rewritten `part` arg each turn it red.

- Three copy-pasted `ObjectNotFound` envelopes are now built once per surface instead of once per call site, and the `VariableDimensionsNotPersisted` diagnostics are no longer retyped at each of its twelve failure branches. The `ObjectNotFound` envelope was 62 literal emissions across 30 worker files; its ten most repeated forms accounted for 23 of them, and 18 of those were exact duplicates of another site - 6 in `HistoryService` (restore, dry-run, list, discard, save, version restore), 10 in `LayoutService` (tree, controls, preview, report, mutator scan, catalog) and 2 more for the KB-revision paths. `nextSteps[]` is the curated recovery contract an LLM client follows without prose-parsing, so a copy-pasted copy is a contract that can drift from the surface it belongs to: an edit to one site's guidance left the other eleven telling the client to do something else. Each surface now has one named builder - `HistoryService.SnapshotObjectNotFound`, `HistoryService.RevisionObjectNotFound`, `LayoutService.VisualObjectNotFound` - with the two history surfaces kept deliberately distinct because their hints name different KB state. The same applied to the twelve `extra` blocks of `VerifyVariableDimensionsPersisted`, which all report the same expected projection: `VariableProjectionExtra`, `VariableProjectionMismatchExtra` and `VariableRequestedProjectionExtra` build the three shapes, so only the message/hint and the observed projection vary per failure. Wire output is unchanged. `ObjectNotFoundEnvelopeTests` pins the resulting envelopes, and both a hint edit and a dropped `name_contains` arg turn it red.
