- Five visual readers in `LayoutService` now resolve their object and its visual context through one prologue, and the `InvalidVisualXml` envelope is written once instead of three times - which is how a real drift turned up. `GetTree`, `FindControls`, `SetProperty`, `GetVisualPreview` and `SetProperties` each did the same ordered chain by hand: no object, then a readable context, and only then the document. That ordering is the contract rather than an incidental shape - a reader that skipped a step would walk a document it never confirmed it could parse - and it was previously enforced only by five separate copies remembering to be identical. The `Document.Root == null` refusal was written out three times behind the same `InvalidVisualXml` code, and the copy in `LayoutService.VisualContext` had already drifted: it dropped "or inspecting the part directly" from the hint and reworded the recovery step to "Diagnoses available visual surfaces" behind a `why` that read as a different instruction. `nextSteps[]` is the recovery contract a client follows without parsing prose, so that is a client-visible divergence behind one error code. Both the chain and the envelope are now `BeginVisualRead` and `InvalidVisualXml`, keeping the fuller of the two wordings, and the `LayoutService.VisualContext` site is switched over with a comment recording what it used to say. The root check is deliberately **not** folded into the prologue: only the two tree walkers need it, and the other three read the document as text and today accept a rootless part, so folding it in would reject input they accept. The fourth `InvalidVisualXml` in that file - a parse failure carrying the exception message - is a different condition and is left alone, with a test asserting it stays distinct. `LayoutReadPrologueTests` pins the call count, the guard ordering, the exact guard conditions and the single definition; weakening either guard with an extra condition turns it red.

- The `BeginReportMutation` source guard was counting something that stopped meaning what it claimed. It asserted that `if (setup.Error != null) return setup.Error;` appears three times, on the reasoning that this line marks the start of a report-mutation prologue. `BeginVisualRead` uses the identical line, so the count rose to eight and the guard - which cannot fail any more - went red on the change that made it meaningless. It now matches the call and its guard as a pair, which says which prologue it belongs to regardless of what the other helper's guard looks like. Inverting the report guard turns it red.

- An entire install-preview subsystem was dead inside `ModuleService` and nothing had noticed. `install` and `install_builtin` both route to `InstallVerified`, which plans through `ModuleInstallFlow` and returns that flow's receipt - and the dryRun preview the capabilities table advertises comes from there, not from here. Four private methods still built their own, older envelopes: `PreviewInstall`, `PreviewInstallByName`, `PackageResultDetails` and `PackageValidationError`. Not one had a caller; the last had none even among the other three. They emitted `ModuleInstallPreview` and `ModuleInstallBuiltInPreview`, which no other code in the repository produces, so no client and no contract document depended on them either. 272 lines are gone and the capability is untouched. What made the drift hard to see is that the dead code looked like a bug: the preview projected `package.description` and the post-install result did not, behind the same key. It was not a bug - both projections belonged to code no caller could reach. Deleting the preview also orphaned four helpers with no test coverage, which went with it, and left two behind: `SelectDependencySource`, a pure preference function written to be testable without a live KB, and `TryGetExportDependencies`, which records that `ModuleContentPart.ExportDependencies` is present on GeneXus 16, 17 and 18. Those two keep their tests and are now asserted to be production-dead *and* still covered, so keeping them is a recorded decision rather than the next reader's unexplained discovery.

- The service-wide reachability check is now a test rather than a script run. `ModuleServiceInstallPathTests` discovers the service files instead of listing them, so a new service cannot opt out by never being named, and asserts that the *only* unreachable private methods anywhere under `Services/` are six named ones. Those six are the advertised-but-unwired gaps already reported - the post-write verification hooks in `genexus_patch` and `genexus_structure`, the WWP resolution and legacy-build paths - so the allowlist keeps them visible and also fails if one is silently deleted. It found a seventh that `scripts/find-unreachable.ps1` did not report: `PatternAnalysisService.FindWWPInstance`, whose only production reference is its own declaration. The reason the script missed it was not its declaration parser, which handles that shape correctly, but the two defects fixed below.

- `scripts/find-unreachable.ps1` was not the only thing that turned out to be wrong about reachability, and the guard for it was wrong twice before it was right. The first version scanned all of `src/` for callers, which included the test project - and the guard named the six expected-unreachable members in its own allowlist, so every one of them counted as referenced and the assertion passed for the wrong reason. Restricting the scan to production sources fixed that. The second version then had its own coverage check satisfied by the six references its own file made to `SelectDependencySource` and `TryGetExportDependencies`, so deleting `SdkMemberCompatibilityTests.cs` - the file that actually tests them - left the guard green. The check now excludes its own file and requires real coverage elsewhere. Both traps are the same one: a guard that mentions a name can satisfy a test that counts mentions of it. All four guards are mutation-checked - re-adding a deleted method, dropping `install_builtin` from the routing line, deleting the real coverage, and restoring a production caller each turn the suite red on a clean compile.

### Fixed

- `scripts/find-unreachable.ps1` was reporting a clean tree while six unreachable members existed, for two independent reasons, and both are fixed. The first is that it counted identifier references across all of `src`, tests included, while reading declarations only from the production projects. A private method was therefore reported only if *nothing at all* named it, so any test that merely mentioned it silenced it completely. That is not hypothetical: the reachability guard above names the six unwired members in its allowlist, and with that one file present the script reported `0` unreachable members. The silence was indistinguishable from a clean tree - the failure mode the tool exists to prevent. The second is that its count maps were plain PowerShell hashtables, which are case-insensitive, and this repository has `PatternAnalysisService.FindWWPInstance` alongside `SaveAsService.FindWwpInstance`: different methods, different classes, differing only in case. Being last-writer-wins, the four references to the second were credited to the first, so the first was reported live. C# identifiers are case-sensitive, so the maps are now `Ordinal` and the tool agrees with the language. The counts are now two maps - production and test - and the report is two buckets, because "unreachable" is two findings with opposite answers: `DEAD`, no production caller and nothing names it, is a deletion candidate; `UNWIRED`, no production caller but a test names it, is an advertised capability whose wiring never landed, or an internal kept alive only by its own tests, and deleting one deletes its coverage. Collapsing them is what turned six reported gaps into six suggested deletions, which is worse than reporting nothing. The self-test grew from 8 declaration-parser cases to 15, adding the classifier itself - a test that only proves the parser cannot catch a classifier that says "dead" about anything a test mentions. All three changes are mutation-checked: restoring case-insensitive maps, collapsing the two buckets, and blanking the test corpus each turn the self-test red or flip the report to `dead=6 unwired=0`. With the tool fixed and the C# test agreeing, the Worker and Gateway have **no dead private members left**; the six unwired ones remain, and are unchanged.

### Changed

- The `resources/read` completion envelope is built once, and its cache lifetime is a constant. `BuildStaticResourceResponse` wrote the whole envelope out in full seven times - `resultType`, `ttlMs`, `cacheScope`, and a `contents` array wrapping a Markdown document - differing only in which document and, for one, the lifetime. `ttlMs` and `cacheScope` are what a client reads to decide whether it may hold the response, so seven copies are seven chances for one resource to disagree with another about that. The envelope is now `ResourceCompletion`, and the lifetime is `PublicResourceTtlMs`, which also replaced the bare literal `3600000` at the four other sites that share the same meaning: `tools/list`, the profile-filtered `tools/list`, `prompts/list`, `resources/list`, `resources/templates/list` and `server/discover`. Health keeps its own `LiveResourceTtlMs` and `private` scope, because a client entitled to cache a health report for an hour is a client reporting a stale Gateway. This is the first Gateway-side duplication pass: the earlier sweeps all required files of at least 1200 lines, and every Gateway file is under that, so the whole side had gone unscanned.

- The fields that were unpinned are now pinned, and the coverage gap is why the duplication survived. The existing tests asserted `resultType == "complete"` and `ttlMs > 0` for the playbook resources and nothing at all about `cacheScope`, the actual lifetime, the MIME type, or the URI the envelope echoes back. `McpResourceEnvelopeTests` drives `McpRouter.Handle` and asserts the emitted envelope for each resource - the two exact-match documents plus one discovered from each prefix-matched family, so the set cannot go stale against a key that no longer exists. It pins health as the only private, short-lived resource in both directions, because "health is the exception" only means something if nothing else drifted into private. The literals are counted with comments stripped: the constant's own documentation quotes `3600000` to explain where it came from, and counting prose reports a definition that is not there.

### Fixed

- `resources/read` echoes the request URI back in `contents[].uri` for four resources and a canonical lowercase literal for three, and the difference is observable. `UnscopeResourceUri` preserves the caller's casing, so a client asking for `GENEXUS://KB/TOOL-HELP/x` gets that exact string back, while the same client asking for `GENEXUS://KB/AGENT-PLAYBOOK` gets `genexus://kb/agent-playbook`. Health, agent-playbook and llm-playbook match on a case-insensitive comparison and then hand a literal to the envelope; the skill, Nexa-reference, gotcha and tool-help families match on a prefix and hand the request. **Not changed here**: normalising either side alters what four resources echo, which is wire output, and choosing which is correct is a call about client expectations rather than a simplification. It is now pinned from both directions so it cannot drift silently, and the helper's documentation records that the inconsistency is deliberate preservation rather than an oversight.

### Changed

- Where the index-state snapshot for a Knowledge Base is decided in one place. A snapshot lives in a per-KB map when its alias resolves and in a single global field when it does not, and `UpdateLastKnownIndexDiagnostics` and `UpdateLastKnownSourceStore` each wrote out all twelve lines of that decision verbatim: resolve the alias, fall back to `ResolveKbAliasForIndexRefresh`, load-or-create, mutate, then write back to whichever side it came from. The value of stating that once is not line count. A writer that resolved the location differently from a reader would put the same KB's state somewhere nothing looks, and that surfaces as a KB whose index never becomes usable - no exception, no log line, just an index that stays Cold. So it is now `MutateLastKnownIndexState`, and callers only say what changed. `InvalidateIndexStateForKb` is deliberately left out of it: it is always called with an explicit alias, has no fallback on purpose, and must never fall through to the global snapshot, because invalidating the global state because a named KB was asked to reindex is a different and wrong operation. That exception is now recorded at the method rather than left for the next reader to "fix" into the shared path.

- The behaviour that consolidation was supposed to preserve is now tested, through the emitted payload rather than the source. `IndexStateMutationStoreTests` drives two new `ForTest` shims - following the six this file already has - and reads the result back with `BuildIndexBlockForTest`, asserting that an update lands on the snapshot for the alias it was given and that the *other* KB, and the global snapshot, do not see it. Two contracts fell out of writing it and are now pinned rather than assumed: the stored `sourceStore` and `cacheValidation` payloads are deep copies, so a caller editing its `JObject` afterwards cannot rewrite already-recorded diagnostics; and these are merge updates, not replacements, so a caller that reports only a resumed position does not erase the checkpoint state the next caller recorded. An absent field is emitted as an explicit JSON null rather than omitted, which is why the "did not leak" assertions test for a `Null` token rather than for a missing key - the opposite test would pass for a missing field and fail for a null one.

### Internal

- One of those rules is pinned structurally, and the reason is worth recording rather than hiding. `InvalidateIndexStateForKb` is distinguished from the shared helper only when `ResolveKbAliasForIndexRefresh` returns something, which needs `_currentKb` or a worker pool holding exactly one open KB - process-level state belonging to the live fixtures that are permanently unavailable here. Adding the fallback to that method changed **nothing** across all 130 index-filtered tests, which is exactly why it needed an assertion rather than a behavioural test: without one, the narrowing is a single refactor away from disappearing silently. The test says so in place, and strips comments before checking, because the method documents the exclusion in place and the explanation necessarily names the thing being excluded. Four mutations are checked, each turning the suite red on a clean compile: writing the snapshot back to the global while reading it from the per-KB map, dropping the deep copy, turning a merge update into an overwrite, and adding the fallback back.

### Fixed

- The stale-result refresh for a lifecycle job is one rule, not two, and the two copies sat in the two places it matters most. `action=result` and `action=status` each independently reconciled the job against the worker and then re-read its stored result, with the same eighteen lines each and only the explanatory comment differing. A drift between them means one action reports a stale result while the other refreshes - which is the failure Issue #27 item 1 describes, and which no single-action test would notice, because each action still looks right on its own. It is now `RefreshLifecycleResultFromWorkerAsync`, and the conditions are named in one place because they are the substance of the rule rather than incidental detail: the reconciliation is unconditional, since a wedged background poller is what leaves a finished build stuck at `running`; the stored result is only re-read when it already carries `newWarnings`, which is what marks it as a warning-bearing snapshot that can be stale rather than a still-forming one; and only once the job is neither `running` nor `queued`, because a live job has nothing final to refresh from. The write stays inside the job's own `SyncRoot` because the poller writes the same field - an unsynchronised write there would be a torn read rather than a visible failure.

- The rule had no coverage at all, and what protects it now says so rather than implying more. The existing lifecycle tests cover `BuildJobResultEnvelope` and the `newWarnings` shaping, not the refresh; and the refresh cannot be exercised without a worker to reconcile against, which places it behind the process-level fixtures that are permanently unavailable in this repository. So `LifecycleResultRefreshRuleTests` pins the clause set structurally - each condition, the order of the reconciliation relative to the check that depends on it, the lock, and the fact that both actions go through the shared rule while neither re-implements it inline. Five mutations are checked and each turns it red: dropping the `queued` guard, dropping the `newWarnings` requirement, writing the result without the lock, moving the reconciliation after the check it feeds, and removing the refresh from `action=status`. The test states the limitation in place, because a structural guard that reads as behavioural coverage is worse than no guard.

### Fixed

- `projection=standard` is now the minimal projection plus more, by construction rather than by comment. `genexus_properties` documents the two projections as a widening pair, and the code said so with a marker comment: `StandardProjectionPropertyNames` spelled the minimal nineteen names out a second time under a `// Minimal set` line marking where the copy ended. Nothing enforced the containment. Adding a name to `MinimalProjectionPropertyNames` and not to the standard list would leave a property that `projection=minimal` returns and `projection=standard` does not, so a caller asking for more detail would get less - and the symptom would be a field missing from one shape of the same object, not an error. The standard set is now built from the minimal one, so the relationship holds by construction.

- The two projections were also written out separately and identically, differing only in the name they echo and the set they filter by. That shape is the client contract: both emit `projection`, `values`, `properties`, and `versionToken` when one was supplied, so a key added to one was silently absent from the other. They now share `BuildProjectionResult`, and the unfiltered full read is deliberately still separate - it does not filter, and it returns the whole cloned result rather than a reconstructed one. The first value seen for a name still wins in `values`, matching the unfiltered path, and that rule is now stated rather than implied by two identical loops.

- `PropertyProjectionSetTests` pins the invariant, the published name lists, and the comparer. The containment is the important one. The names are pinned in **both** directions, because a projection is published output: a name leaving the standard list, or the minimal set changing at all, is a wire change and now has to be written down in the test rather than deleted from a literal. The comparer is pinned behaviourally rather than by reading the declaration, because it is what makes the duplicate SDK spellings work - both sets list `Picture` and `ATT_PICTURE`, and the standard set adds `IsNullable` and `ALLOWNULL`, because GeneXus exposes the same property under different capitalisations on different objects. A case-sensitive set would silently stop returning one of each pair: a property that vanishes from a projection with no error and no other symptom. Four mutations are checked: rebuilding the standard set independently, making the copied set case-sensitive, removing a standard-metadata name, and filtering the standard projection by the minimal set.

### Fixed

- The WebForm save diagnostics name their binding flags instead of rewriting them, and its three exact-signature SDK probes go through the shared resolver. A probe that omits `FlattenHierarchy` reports an SDK capability as unavailable - the SDK moves helper members between a type and its base class between GeneXus majors, and the hierarchy flag is the only thing that finds the base-class form. `SdkMemberProbe` exists to make that impossible to get wrong by accident, and the diagnostics were spelling the same four-flag expression out at six call sites instead of naming it: six chances to drop one. They now use a new `SdkMemberProbe.InstanceAnyVisibility`, which is deliberately *not* `StaticOrInstanceAnyVisibility` - it excludes `Static`, because these probes invoke a member on a part instance, and `GetMethods` returning a same-named static overload first would let a probe report success for a call that did not do what the caller meant.

- The three-argument `LoadModelEntityOutput` lookup was written out twice, in two different diagnostics, as the same hand-written predicate listing the same three parameter types. A probe that answers differently for the same part is a diagnostic that reports two different things depending on which code path ran. Both now go through `SdkMemberProbe.Resolve`, together with the distinct four-argument `LoadVersionIndependentOutput` probe that sat beside them - leaving one hand-written next to two converted is the inconsistency worth removing. The three lookups that stay hand-written are the ones selecting by arity or by parameter *name* (`SaveHeader()`, `SaveHeader(SavePreferences)`, `Save(<preferences>)`): their parameter type is discovered at runtime rather than known, so a typed resolver cannot express them. Two side effects worth recording rather than hiding. Adopting the resolver means an exactly-duplicated signature - the same one declared on the type and on a base class - now reports *absent* instead of binding whichever member `GetMethods` happened to return first, which is the resolver's documented policy and the safer of the two. And the eight `GetProperty` sites keep their own expression without the hierarchy flag, because a property lookup is a different operation; a test asserts the boundary so a future reader does not "fix" them onto a flag set that would change which property is found.

- `SdkMemberProbeBindingFlagTests` pins the new constant's meaning behaviourally, against a purpose-built type with a public, a private, an inherited and two static members. A test that only compared the two constants' values would still pass if both were wrong in the same way; asserting what each flag set actually finds does not. The constants are also asserted to differ by exactly `Static`, as a difference rather than as two literals, so adding a flag to either is a deliberate act. Three mutations are checked: giving the instance set `Static`, dropping `FlattenHierarchy` from it, and re-inlining the duplicated probe by hand.

### Changed

- `genexus_read full` assembles each object part in one place, and the code-versus-metadata decision it was hiding is now explicit. `ObjectService.ReadFullObject` dispatches on object type, and every branch repeated the same six lines - read the part, skip it if blank, store it under a key, append it to the combined source - twelve times across Procedure, the five UI types, Transaction, Table, SDT and API, in two shapes. The branches were the only thing holding the two shapes together: eleven parts were code, and a Transaction's, a Table's and an SDT's `Structure` part was metadata, reported under `parts` but deliberately kept out of the combined source because it is a DSL description of the object rather than authored code. Written out by hand that decision was the *absence* of one line in an otherwise identical block, so a reader had to reconstruct which parts were code - and a stray append would put a DSL description inside the combined source of a Transaction, which is the kind of wrong that reads as plausible. It is now `AddReadPart`, whose `isCode` argument says so at each site. The one genuinely per-type read is untouched: the five UI types fall back to `SDEEvents` when `Events` is absent, which is why their branch cannot be a plain list of part names.

- The "this part is not readable" diagnostic is one function. An unreadable visual part and an unresolvable pattern instance are different failures with different codes, messages, hints and recovery steps, but both attached the same `extra` block, whose `availableParts` list is precisely what the client is told to recover with - pick a part this object actually exposes. A drift between the two copies would leave one error advertising a way out that the other does not, behind the same field name. It is now `PartUnavailableExtra`, which also means the available-parts lookup, an SDK call, is evaluated once per error rather than by two independent reads of the same object. The twelve other `availableParts` sites in the Worker are left alone: they assign into an existing result or draw from a different source, and none of them shares this shape.

### Internal

- `ReadFullObjectSharedPartTests` pins the rule rather than the shape, because the shape was never the risk. The helper is private and the method needs a live SDK object, so the decision is asserted at the call sites: `Structure` is metadata for all three types that read it, no other part is, and the `SDEEvents` fallback appears once. Four mutations are checked: making a `Structure` part append to the combined source, dropping the blank-part guard so empty parts are stored, appending metadata parts too, and removing a field from the unavailable-parts diagnostic.

### Fixed

- The Gateway's two "has this operation finished?" questions are no longer the same three literals written out three times. `OperationTracker` had an `IsTerminalOperationStatus` helper used by the three status-poll paths, and each of the two cancellation paths hand-rolled the identical three comparisons. The two are not the same question, and that is the part worth recording. A record can only ever hold `Completed`, `Failed` or `Cancelled` as terminal states - those are what the tracker assigns, and nothing assigns them back. The polled helper additionally recognises `NotFound`, which is the envelope the tracker emits for an id it does not know rather than a state a record holds, and `Stalled`, which **no path in the class can produce**: the only stalled producer in the Gateway writes that into a worker payload, and the background-job registry has its own lowercase job status read elsewhere. Had the cancellation paths simply called the polled helper, a not-found-shaped status would have made a cancel request report "already terminal" for a still-live operation. They cannot get that status today, so this is not a live bug - but it is exactly the kind of distinction that erodes, and it is now `IsTerminalRecordState`, named for what it answers.

- Writing the tests for that found the trap in the class's own API, which is worth recording because it is silent rather than loud. `StartOperation` takes a `requestId` and returns an `operationId`, and `CompleteFromWorker` is keyed by the *request* id. Passing the operation id to it leaves the record stranded at `Running` forever, with no error - which is the same failure `LinkRequest` exists to prevent on a retried request. The test helper now returns both halves as a pair, with the reason in place, so the next reader does not have to rediscover it.

- `OperationTrackerTerminalStatusTests` drives the real tracker through its public surface for the behavioural half: a new operation is `Running`, cancelling reaches `Cancelled` and is idempotent, a cancel *requested* is recorded without acting, and a `Failed` or `Completed` operation is terminal for both cancellation paths. The split predicate itself is pinned at the source, because the behavioural tests cannot reach a status this tracker never produces. Three mutations are checked, each turning it red: one cancellation path stops consulting the predicate, the record-level predicate loses a term, and a term is misspelled so a failure looks live.

### Fixed

- The lifecycle result and warning payloads compute their page window once. They are the same envelope around a different collection - one keyed `items`, one keyed `warnings` - and each was computing the window itself: the same clamps, the same `skip`/`has_more` arithmetic, the same bounds-checked loop, and the same `_meta.pagination` block. That arithmetic decides which page a client sees and whether `has_more` tells it to ask again, so two copies is two chances for the two payloads to disagree about the page they are showing. Clamping, slicing and the meta block are now `ClampPage`, `PageOf` and `PaginationMeta`. The two payloads keep their own collection key, which is part of what each caller reads, and a test asserts they differ in exactly that.

- The batch-edit completion envelope is built in one place, which also removes a difference that looked deliberate. Three sites produced it - an empty change list, the transactional path and the per-change path - and the empty-list case reported a literal `0` for the duration where the other two read the stopwatch. That is not a meaningful distinction: no stopwatch has run at that point, and a client reading `duration` gets `0` from one path and an elapsed value from the others with nothing to tell it why. All three now hand a stopwatch to `BatchEditCompleted`.

- `BatchServicePaginationTests` holds the window contract by driving both builders through the same inputs and comparing what they report, across the boundaries rather than the middle: page 0 and negative pages, page sizes of 0 and negative, an oversized page size, a page past the end, a null list and an exactly-full final page. `has_more` is checked against whether more items actually exist rather than against a literal, so the field cannot claim there is another page when there is not. The source-level half pins the bounds themselves, and that split is deliberate: raising the page-size cap from 200 to 500 **passed every behavioural case**, because none of them requests a page large enough to tell the two apart. The cases cover the clamping *behaviour*; the source assertion covers the *number*, and says why. Four mutations are checked: `has_more` without the page offset, the skip losing its page term, the cap changing, and the empty-list duration literal returning.

### Fixed

- The two readers of an object text document agree on what a document is. One reads the source, one reads the named parts, and each was doing the whole split itself: the emptiness check, the newline normalisation, the header split, the two-part validation, the brace search and the balance check. **The three refusal messages were duplicated with them**, which is the part that matters: a client parsing malformed text could be told "Object header must contain type and name." by the source reader and something else by the parts reader for the same document, and nothing in a parse failure tells a caller which reader rejected it. The split is now `TrySplitObjectDocument`, returning the body raw so each reader keeps its own interpretation of it - the source reader strips a leading `#Source`, the parts reader walks the body's own sections.

- `ObjectTextDocumentSplitTests` asserts the agreement directly: the same document is accepted by both or refused by both, and refused with the *same sentence* rather than merely failing twice, across empty and whitespace-only text, headers that do not split, and four kinds of unbalanced body. Newline normalisation is checked as shared behaviour - a CRLF document must round-trip through both readers, because if it were left to each, one would accept a Windows-authored file and the other would reject it. Three format consequences are now pinned that were previously implicit: a name containing spaces is kept whole, because the header splits on the first separator with a limit of two, and the source reader's output deliberately carries the `#End` marker and later sections because it is less structured than the parts reader. Those are recorded rather than asserted away - the point of the tests is that the two readers differ, not that they agree. Four mutations are checked: weakening the brace balance check, dropping the newline normalisation, removing the header split's limit, and having the parts reader reword one refusal.

### Fixed

- `genexus_refactor` gives the same advice when a code block is not found, whichever refactor refused. Three call sites built the `CodeBlockNotFound` envelope - extract-procedure before and after the write, and extract-subroutine - and the duplication mattered less than the drift. They shared the code, the message, the recovery step and the target, and **the hint said different things**: "ensure the code matches the source verbatim" against "ensure whitespace and formatting match verbatim", with the subroutine version adding "in any source part of the target object". A caller reading a refusal to decide what to fix therefore got different advice from the same failure depending on which refactor it called, and *neither* version described the condition actually checked - the caller passes a block and the refactor searches every source part, so "any source part" is the accurate statement of all three. That is the wording kept, and it is now the only one. The `SourceObjectNotFound` refusal, which two extractions and a third refactor each wrote out, is the same treatment.

- The other `ObjectNotFound` envelopes in that file are left alone, and the boundary is asserted rather than left to judgement. They are about a *target* rather than a source: they tell the caller to disambiguate by type and route to `genexus_list_objects` for different reasons. Folding them into the source-object refusal would tell a caller who already named the source explicitly to go looking for a different object. `RefactorRefusalEnvelopeTests` pins both halves - the shared wording, its call-site count, and the three distinct target-object hints that must stay distinct. Three mutations are checked: the hint reverting to the narrower drifted wording, a call site re-inlining the envelope, and the source-object recovery step being reworded.

### Fixed

- A print-block mutation's read-back states the two-attribute match once. After a rename, add or delete commits, the Gateway re-reads the report XML to decide whether the change actually reached disk, and it looked for a `PrintBlock` element whose `Name` **or** `ControlName` matched - a two-clause predicate written out at five sites. Both attributes are needed because GeneXus exposes a print block's name under one or the other depending on the major, so a site checking only one would report a completed mutation as unverified: the exact failure the read-back exists to catch, caused by the check itself. Five copies is five chances for the fifth to be the wrong one, and the symptom is a mutation that claims it failed when it succeeded. It is now `FindPrintBlockByName` and its negative `ContainsPrintBlock`.

- The post-commit repair two mutations did by hand is one method. Rename and add each re-resolved the object, re-read its report surface, re-normalised the source commands and flushed if anything changed - a *repair*, not a rollback, since the transaction has already committed. The two failing differently would be indistinguishable from the SDK misbehaving in one specific way, and the next print-block mutation should not have to decide again whether to attempt a repair. `DeletePrintBlock` deliberately does not call it: proving a block is gone needs no normalisation, and normalising a document in which a block was expected to have disappeared repairs nothing. The repair stays silent by design - a verification failure is the caller's answer already, and a repair that cannot complete does not change it - and a test asserts it neither reports nor throws, so a future change that starts doing either is deliberate.

- `PrintBlockReadBackTests` checks the match rule against real XML rather than source text: both attribute spellings, both present together, three case variations, a non-matching block, and a report with no print blocks at all - which is the delete path's success case, so the predicate has to be safe on an empty document rather than throwing. Three mutations are checked, each turning it red on a clean compile: the finder dropping the `ControlName` spelling, the repair building an envelope, and delete attempting the repair.

### Fixed

- A route rename states the offset-ordered rewrite once. Renaming a route rewrites the method name and the path into the same copy of the API's source block, and the order the two replacements happen in is the whole content of the operation: the recorded offsets are positions in the original text, so replacing the later one first shifts the earlier one's position and the second replacement lands in the wrong place. That is not a compile error and not a thrown exception - it is a written file with something wrong in the middle of it. The rewrite was written out twice, in `CloneApiRoute` and `CloneApiRouteTo`, and the two copies differed only in where the new names came from. `CloneApiRoute` now computes the names and delegates.

- The route write path's version-conflict refusal is one envelope. It checks for a conflict twice - once against the snapshot it planned from, and again after re-reading the API - and both refusals were written out in full, identical except for *which snapshot's token they reported*. That difference is the point of the check, so it is now an argument rather than a coincidence of which variable each site had in scope: the second reports the freshly-read token, so a caller retrying with it is comparing against what the API looks like now. The third `VersionConflict` in the file stays its own thing, because "the API could not be re-read" is a read failure rather than a change and carries no `expectedVersion`; folding it in would tell a caller the API changed when the truth is that it could not be read.

- `ApiRouteSourceRewriteTests` derives its fixtures from `ParseApiRoutes` on real API source instead of building them by hand, so a change to the parser's recorded offsets is what it would notice. It asserts that those offsets address the two spans in the original text, that parsed routes always put the path span first - which is what makes one side of the branch the live one - and the exact rewritten text for both sides of the branch, since apply either order to both shapes and one exact string no longer holds. Three mutations are checked, each turning it red on a clean compile: always replacing the method span first, `CloneApiRoute` re-spelling the rewrite instead of delegating, and the second conflict check reporting the stale token.

### Fixed

- The dispatcher's unknown-action refusal is one envelope. Every tool that takes an action ends on the same refusal for an action it does not serve - the same `UnknownAction` code, the same two sentences with the tool's name dropped in, and the same next step. Five handlers carried that written out in full. It is shared because this is the one shape that ends every failed discovery attempt: a caller that guessed an action is told, in a fixed form, that nothing was written and where to look instead, and an agent reads it programmatically. Two handlers disagreeing about that means one of them is the odd one out, and which one is not obvious to whoever reads the transcript afterwards. A sixth handler now inherits the shape instead of deciding again whether to spell it right.

- `OrientWelcomeNextSteps` states the shared next step once. The dispatcher's own unknown-*combination* refusal - which is a different condition, since the pair named neither a known tool nor a known action - reaches for the same place, so the step is built in one place for both. It is built per call rather than held in a field: a `JArray` is mutable and is attached to the response, so a shared instance would let one envelope's next step be rewritten by whoever held a different response, which would only surface under concurrency.

- `DispatcherUnknownActionTests` calls the production helper rather than restating the shape, and pins the set of five tool names so adding or renaming a handler is a deliberate diff rather than a silent change to what a caller is told to call. It also asserts that the envelope's top-level fields are exactly `error`, `status` and `target` - a refusal that quietly grew a `persisted` or `retryable` field would change what a client infers from it - and that the action is echoed verbatim, including the translation pass `Err` runs over the message. Four mutations are checked, each turning it red on a clean compile: the next step cached in a field, the hint dropping the `genexus_` prefix, the action echoed lowercased, and a sixth site hand-rolling the block.

  One of those four is caught only structurally, and the test says so. Caching the array in a field leaves every behavioural assertion green, because the helper returns serialised JSON and each caller re-parses its own copy - so a shared instance is invisible from the output. The content half is behavioural and the instance half is a source check, and both are needed: content alone would pass for a single shared instance, and the absence of a field would pass for two hand-written copies that happen to match.

### Fixed

- The stale-WWP-`PatternInstance` refusal is one envelope. Six operations refuse this way - five under the per-target lock in the `WwpActionService` partials, plus the router path - and each wrote the whole envelope out. What made the copies worth removing is the `extra`, not the code: its two fields are the entire retry contract, because a caller re-runs with `expectedVersion` and compares against `currentVersion`. Six hand-written copies of a contract read programmatically is six chances for one to drop a field or rename it, and the result is a caller who can do nothing but re-read the whole pattern. The per-operation message stays an argument, because naming the attribute, table, tab, form action or component is what tells an operator which stage refused.

- Only the envelope is shared, never the comparison that triggers it. `add_grid_attribute` requires a version and refuses up front with `ExpectedVersionRequired`, so its check is unconditional; the other four treat an absent version as "no check asked for". That is a stricter contract, not an oversight, and folding the condition in would silently make the four lenient ones strict.

- `add_form_action` no longer captures a resolved object it never reads. It was the one path naming the `BuildPatternPartEnvelope` out-parameter into a local, and the local was written and never read again - the other four discard it.

- `WwpStaleObjectEnvelopeTests` pins the contract per file and per operation, and asserts the triggering comparison stays at each call site with the grid/rest difference intact - so a future edit does not unify them by accident. It also pins the recorded decision that the surrounding read-resolve-compare prologue stays inline, so that is a visible state rather than something re-litigated or undone because the refusal beside it now looks shared. Five mutations are checked, each turning it red on a clean compile: the envelope reporting the wrong current version, a partial spelling the envelope out inline again, grid losing its up-front version requirement, one of the four lenient sites becoming unconditional, and the unused local returning.

  One detail worth stating because it cost time to find: the version fields land at the **envelope's top level**, not inside `error`. `Err` merges its `extra:` argument into the envelope rather than into the error object, so a caller reading the retry contract from the obvious place gets `null` and no explanation. The tests assert that location explicitly, and that `error.extra` stays absent.

### Fixed

- "Which KB is this call about" is one rule. Five services accept a `kbPathOverride` and then need a KB path: four copied the same four-line private `ResolveKbPath`, and `genexus_screenshot` publish inlined the same logic in a slightly different shape. It is now `EffectiveKbPath.Resolve`, which states the three clauses once - an explicitly named path is used verbatim and wins over session state; otherwise the answer is whichever KB is open, and no KB at all is `null` rather than an error; and it never throws. That last clause is why the duplication mattered: callers use the result to decide whether to refuse, so a resolution failure surfacing as an exception would replace a clear `NoKbOpen` envelope with a stack trace in a place that has no handler for one.

  The override is returned unchecked - not normalised, trimmed, or tested for existence - so a caller that passes a relative path, or a path for a KB that does not exist yet, gets exactly what it passed and refuses in its own words.

  The `try` around the lookup is currently redundant, because `KbService.GetKbPath()` already catches internally and cannot throw today. It stays: this rule promises its callers it never throws, and `GetKbPath` is a different class with different callers that may reasonably want the exception. If it stopped swallowing, this would still hold. `EffectiveKbPathTests` pins both halves structurally and says in its doc comment that no behavioural test for the throwing case exists or is claimed - reaching it needs a `KbService` that throws from a sealed type's own state.

- `EffectiveKbPathTests` calls the production helper rather than restating the precedence rule, because a test that reimplemented it would pass with a wrong rule in production. It also asserts no caller reaches `GetKbPath` directly any more, since the point of the shared rule is that the precedence and the no-throw promise are the same everywhere. Five mutations are checked, each turning it red on a clean compile: the override losing precedence, the no-throw promise dropped, one service growing its own copy, one reaching `GetKbPath` directly, and the inner `GetKbPath` stopping swallowing.

### Fixed

- The WWP snapshot gate states its five terms once. A WorkWithPlus mutation may only proceed when it captured everything it needs to prove what it changed - the object's own native bytes, its resolved parent, that parent's WebForm text, and before-snapshots of both the PatternInstance and the WebForm. Five terms is enough that dropping one is easy and invisible: the term disappears, every test still passes because nothing exercised a missing snapshot, and the next real failure is a mutation applied without the state needed to roll it back. The check was written out identically at all five operation sites; it is now `WwpSnapshotsIncomplete`, and `WwpSnapshotGateTests` checks all five terms individually - which a source-level check cannot do, since it cannot tell a dropped term from a renamed one.

  **Only the condition is shared, never the refusal that follows it.** The five refusals are three different contracts: two report only `snapshot` and `persisted`, three add four diagnostic booleans, and `replace_web_component` reports them through `errorExtra`, so they land inside `error` rather than at the envelope's top level. Same code, same intent, two different JSON shapes - unifying them would change where a client reads those fields from. Each site keeps its own, and the tests pin that they stayed apart so a future merge is a decision rather than an accident.

- `SnapshotBundle` is `internal` rather than `private`, so the gate can take it. The rule that decides whether a mutation may proceed is only worth stating once if something can check that none of its five terms went missing, and the SDK type it needs has no constructor reachable from a test. Its fields were already `internal`.

  The bundle is dereferenced rather than null-guarded, deliberately: `CaptureSnapshots` always constructs one, and a throw inside it happens at the call site before the gate. Adding `?.` would imply a case that cannot occur and would quietly convert a crash into a refusal saying the snapshots were unavailable, which is a different diagnosis. Pinned by a test.

### Fixed

- **The source-comment stripper used by the source-shape guards could delete code, so some of those guards were weaker than they looked.** Each of thirteen Worker test classes carried its own copy of a helper that removed block comments with `/\*.*?\*/` under `Singleline`, and four Gateway test classes carried the same one. That assumes every `/*` opens a comment, which breaks on a file containing `/*` inside a string literal: the match then runs to the next `*/` *anywhere in the file*. Measured on `ObjectService.cs`, it returned 199,096 characters for a 298,344-character file - a third of it, including a call site added minutes earlier.

  The failure mode is worth stating precisely, because it is worse than a weak assertion. These guards ask "is this code present?" and "does this appear once?". A stripper that deletes code turns the first into an automatic yes and leaves the second answering a question about whatever survived. Nothing fails, and the guard still looks like it is covering the change.

  It is now one `RepoSource.WithoutComments`, a scanner that tracks line comments, block comments, and regular, verbatim and interpolated literals - and blanks comment characters in place rather than removing them, so lengths, line counts and offsets all survive. Seventeen copies deleted, across both test projects. `RepoSourceCommentStrippingTests` pins the property directly: length and line count preserved, code preserved, prose gone, offsets unmoved, and no production file shortened - over the whole tree, since the hazard is not specific to one file. Two mutations are checked: degrading the scanner back to the naive regex turns 11 of its 13 tests red, and removing literal tracking turns 4 red.

  All seventeen repaired guards were then re-run. Every one passes unchanged, so none had been silently passing; `WwpSnapshotGateTests` was failing *because* of this and now passes.

- The open-KB next step is stated once. Six tools refuse for want of an open KB, and each wrote out the step pointing at `genexus_kb action=open` - in four different formats. The tool name and the action are not the caller's to choose: a next step naming a tool that does not exist, or spelling the action differently, strands exactly the caller who is already lost, and here the caller is an agent choosing its next move from what the tool told it. So `KbOpenNextStep.Step` states those two facts and leaves the substance per-tool: the `why`, which is what makes a step usable rather than generic, and any extra arguments. `db_info` shows a `path` placeholder; the action wins over a caller's own extra args so the step cannot be made to ask for something other than opening a KB.

  The refusals themselves are deliberately not shared. The same condition is reported under two codes - `NoKbOpen` in four places and `KbNotOpen` in four others - with nine spellings of the message between them. That is a real inconsistency and it is left alone: a client matches on the code, so collapsing them would change what existing callers see. The tests pin that both codes are still in use, so the decision stays visible.

  Five mutations are checked, each turning it red on a clean compile: the step naming the wrong tool, the wrong action, extra args overwriting the action, a site reverting to its own step, and the helper growing an envelope.

### Fixed

- The source-shape test helpers are defined once. `Count` was a private copy in 24 test classes, `MethodBody` in 14 and `Normalise` in 3, across both test projects. They are now `SourceAssert`, linked into both. A copied helper is a helper that can drift, and drift is invisible in both directions at once: a stricter copy fails tests that should pass, and a looser one passes tests that should fail - the second being the one that hides a real divergence.

  Two of the fourteen `MethodBody` copies differed from the other twelve only in the wording of an unreachable throw, and are now one. The thirteenth is left alone on purpose and pinned as deliberately different: it locates a member by *name* through a regex and returns its body with the braces removed, where the shared one takes a declaration prefix - so an overload or return type is part of what gets pinned - and returns the member whole. Swapping either for the other would silently change what its assertions read.

- **`SourceAssert.Count` no longer loops forever on an empty needle.** The search advances by the needle's length, so an empty one never moves. All 24 copies had this, and none had ever been called that way - which is exactly why it survived in twenty test classes. It now refuses an empty needle, because a count of `haystack.Length + 1` would read like a finding about the code rather than about the assertion.

- **`SourceAssert.MethodBody` skips string literals while counting braces.** A body containing `var t = "{";` holds an unmatched brace, so the previous behaviour never returned to depth zero and the extracted "body" ran on through every member after it - turning an assertion about the body into one about the rest of the file. All 13 copies had this; none of their tests happened to contain a brace inside a string, so none noticed.

  Both are documented as load-bearing rather than as polish, and `SourceAssertSingleSourceTests` covers them along with the single-sourcing itself. Four mutations are checked, each turning it red on a clean compile: `Count` becoming case-insensitive, the empty-needle guard stopping throwing, `MethodBody` ignoring literals, and `MethodBody` stopping at the first closing brace.

- Two source-shape guards asserted the sentences an envelope is made of rather than the envelope's shape, which charges a reword tax to buy no protection. A count of `private static string CodeBlockNotFound(string sourceObjectName)` and a count of its three call sites stop a fourth divergent copy; a count of the hint text stops the same thing while also failing on any copy-edit, and it protects nothing the counts do not. `RefactorRefusalEnvelopeTests` and `LayoutReadPrologueTests` are rewritten onto code-structure needles - declarations, call sites, argument names, code literals - and a test that existed only to pin wording is deleted, with the judgement it recorded kept as prose where a reader will find it. `RefactorRefusalEnvelopeTests` goes 100 lines to 74, 32 assertions to 12 and 5 tests to 2, all 12 needles structural; `LayoutReadPrologueTests` goes 159 to 128, 30 assertions to 24 and 9 tests to 8. Two mutations are checked on a clean compile, each turning one red: a refactor call site rebuilding the envelope inline, and a second `InvalidVisualXml` builder carrying the same error code.

  This does not apply everywhere. Of the 451 source-text assertion call sites in this branch's test files, the ones reading a user-facing sentence are a small minority - `LayoutReadPrologueTests` had four in thirty. An earlier estimate that put the figure near 219 is not reproducible by any mechanical classification of the files, and is withdrawn rather than acted on.

- `McpResponse.Err` now says where its fields land. `extra` is added at the envelope's top level, beside `status` and `target`, while `errorExtra` is added inside `error` - so the retry contract of a stale-object refusal is read as `response["expectedVersion"]` and not `response["error"]["expectedVersion"]`, and reading the wrong one yields `null` with nothing to explain it. Nothing at a call site indicates which of the two you are passing, and the split means two operations refusing the same way can emit different JSON shapes for one error code. Both at once is deliberate and legitimate - a refusal reporting diagnostics about the failure and the operational state it left behind needs both places, and `genexus_module`'s install failure is the precedent. Unifying the two would change what published clients already read, so it stays a contract decision; the placement is documented at the signature until it is taken.

- `EffectiveKbPath` and `KbOpenNextStep` keep the reasons and lose the narration. `EffectiveKbPath` carried 60 lines of documentation for 6 lines of code. What survives is what a reader cannot derive: that an explicit path wins because a caller naming a KB is asking about that KB, that the method never throws because its callers use the result to decide whether to refuse and a resolution failure arriving as an exception would replace a clear `NoKbOpen` with a stack trace where no handler exists, and that a `null` service is tolerated because these callers are constructed without one in tests. `KbOpenNextStep` keeps the same three, plus why the `why` and extra arguments stay per-tool - "open the target KB before deleting" is what makes the step useful instead of generic - and records that the refusals it serves still differ in code and wording on purpose, since a client matches on the code.

- **A failed release preflight reported no reason at all, and `release-doctor.ps1` threw instead of diagnosing.** Fixes https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/348. Two defects had to be fixed together, and fixing either alone was worse than not fixing it. `Get-PreflightPhaseLogPaths` probed `$Phase.PSObject.Properties['stdoutPath']`, which cannot see the key of an `[ordered]` phase - the shape every production call site passes - so its loop emitted nothing; and `@(...) | Where-Object {...}` is not an array literal but an array subexpression feeding a second pipeline stage, so an empty first stage collapses the whole pipeline to `$null` and `.Count` on it throws under the StrictMode the script inherits from `gx-version-catalog.ps1`. That throw happened inside the `Write-Error` argument, so it took the entire line with it - phase name, reason and log paths - replaced by a property-not-found message pointing at the caller. The summary written a line earlier is correct and complete, so the evidence existed and the operator was never shown it. The probe now reads through `Get-GxMcpReleaseFieldValue`, a dual-shape accessor added beside the existing presence test, and `@()` wraps the whole pipeline. `release-doctor.ps1` had the same blindness plus two more: its `Get-OptionalValue` duplicated the probe, and it read `.Count` off a `$null` sentinel that an empty `phases: []` left behind, while a summary with no `phases` key produced `@($null)` whose Count is 1 rather than 0, so the filter ran and evaluated `.status` on the null. It throws today for all three shapes, and the doctor is the script `docs/RELEASE.md` tells an operator to run first when a preflight is interrupted. The aggregate's failing-phase hints had the identical collapse and were unreachable only because the per-phase hint threw first.

  The suite missed all of it for a reason worth recording: the tests only ever built a phase with `ConvertFrom-Json`, producing a `PSCustomObject`, whose keys *do* surface through `PSObject.Properties`. The path tested and the path in use were disjoint. A guard at `test-release-preflight.ps1:360` used the same blind probe and could therefore never fire. Six regression cases now cover the production `[ordered]` shape with logs, a reused phase with no keys, keys present but null, and three degenerate doctor summaries. The doctor's cases deliberately run against the real repo root rather than a temp fixture, because the doctor inherits StrictMode by dot-sourcing the version catalog inside a `try`, and without it `$null.Count` quietly returns 0 instead of throwing - so against a fixture the buggy code passes and the regression is invisible. That inheritance is itself part of the defect and is noted where the test depends on it. Two mutations confirmed red: restoring the pre-fix body, and restoring the doctor's sentinel. A third attempt was discarded rather than reported - it had overwritten the line that emits the value, so it modelled code that never emits rather than the old code, and the assertion that caught it was the wrong one. `docs/release_protocol.md` claimed the three `Write-Error` sites cite log paths and that a never-ran phase carries `null`; the first never held, the second is wrong because a reused phase omits the keys, and the first is corrected in place.

- **`avgQueueWaitMs` now reports the queue the Worker measured, and a cold start is no longer presented as queue delay.** Fixes https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/355. The gateway computed the field as the interval between stamping a request and handing it to a Worker - admission, Worker acquisition and cold-start waiting - and never read `telemetry.queueWaitMs`, the delay the Worker itself measured, even though `sdkMs`, `transformMs` and `serializeMs` were all read from that same object. Two failures came out of that. A Worker genuinely queued behind other work reported 7000 ms of delay and the summary read 0, because its pre-send interval happened to be near zero, so a busy shared Worker looked idle; and a Worker with no queue at all, whose wait was entirely cold-start, reported that wait as queue time. The Worker measurement is now mapped, and the gateway-side interval is reported separately as `avgAdmissionMs` rather than folded into the queue.

  `ToolLatencyStats.Record`'s `queueWaitMs` became nullable so a call that reported no queue figure is distinguishable from one reporting zero, and `avgQueueWaitMs` is averaged over the calls that reported one, with the denominator exposed as `queueWaitSamples`. A response that never reached telemetry reporting - an error before dispatch - was previously averaged in as an idle queue. A measured zero still counts as measured. `RecordToolLatency` is `internal` so the mapping itself can be tested: every existing test fed phase numbers straight into `ToolLatencyStats.Record`, which is precisely why a wrong phase-to-field mapping could not be observed. Four tests cover the 7000 ms acceptance case in both the shared and the isolated telemetry envelope, cold start not reported as queue, an absent measurement not counted as an idle queue, and the other Worker timings still read from the same telemetry after the extraction moved. Two mutations confirmed red on a clean compile: dropping the `queueWaitMs` read, and restoring the pre-send interval as the queue. A third attempt to build the second mutant did not apply, and was discarded rather than reported as a pass.

  `avgQueueWaitMs` keeps its name, type and place in the summary, so existing telemetry consumers are unaffected; what changes is what it averages over, plus two additive fields beside it. Not verified: the mapping is correct by construction and by test, but no live KB was available to confirm a real queueing Worker reports what it should.

- **The live ProcessSmoke lane now runs from `GXMCP_TEST_KB` alone, against a real KB.** The harness forwards the test KB to the Gateway through `GX_CONFIG_PATH` and deliberately does not pass `GXMCP_TEST_KB` on, so that startup warmup and the configured default do not race to spawn the same Worker. Nothing set `GX_CONFIG_PATH`, so the Gateway fell back to `publish/config.json` - the neutral config `build.ps1` writes deliberately without an `Environment.KBPath` - logged `No Knowledge Base is open` and exited during `initialize`. Every live test saw that as `IOException: The pipe is being closed`. It was invisible while the KB was unset, because the whole lane skipped, and invisible again to anyone who set `GXMCP_TEST_KB` without also discovering the config variable: 28 tests that had never executed reported as a dead pipe rather than as a missing config. The harness now defaults `GX_CONFIG_PATH` to the repository config, which already declares the test KB; an explicit value still wins.

  Measured against `C:\kbs\KBTeste` (GeneXus 18.0.10, native-sdk driver): the lane goes from **10 passed / 28 skipped** to **32 passed / 2 failed / 4 skipped**, and with the defaulting disabled it collapses back to 10 passed / 24 failed, which is the mutation check - 14 failures that were a dead pipe become 14 tests that really exercise the Worker against the KB.

  Two failures remain and neither is a regression. `TeamDevelopmentPendingList_PreservesEarlierMcpWriteAfterLaterWrite` raises a runtime dynamic-skip because the KB is not linked to GeneXus Team Development, and xunit v2 has no runtime skip, so the skip is reported as a failure. `EventsPatch_requires_complete_object_save_and_rejects_stale_version` is refused by the product with `ObjectSaveIsolationUnverified` - "Isolated Events save requires both profile-owned KB and version pins" - which is that guard working as designed against a KB that does not meet its preconditions. Both need a linked disposable KB, not a code change.

- **`genexus_properties action=get` with a single `name` no longer refuses every read with `InvalidBatchTargets`.** Fixes https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/331. The batch guard was `if (args["targets"] != null)`, but for a `JObject` built from JSON a property that is *absent* reads back as C# `null` while a property written as `"targets": null` reads back as a `JValue` of type `Null` - a live object. The guard therefore took its "the caller supplied this" branch for an explicit null, and the next test, `is not JArray`, is also true of that null, so a single-object read was refused with a message about a `targets` the request never contained - naming the very form the caller had used. Callers send it that way routinely: an LLM composing a call from a tool schema tends to serialise every declared property and leave the unused ones null. Four sites had the shape, and all four were wrong in a different way: this one hard-failed; `genexus_edit_and_build` entered its patch-normalisation branch for `"patch": null`, set `mode=patch`, and then matched neither the object nor the string shape, selecting patch mode for a request carrying no patch; `genexus_write` rejected a valid style edit whose `properties` was null as "must be a JSON object"; and the atomic-authoring plan reported a `rules` set the caller never sent. They now share `JsonUtil.IsSupplied`, which tests the token's type rather than its reference, and `ExplicitNullArgumentTests` pins the predicate's behaviour and each call site's use of it. Seven tests, two mutations each confirmed red on a clean compile: the predicate degraded to a plain null test, and the dispatcher call site reverted with the predicate left correct - the second is the one that matters, since the behaviour tests all call `IsSupplied` directly and would otherwise have stayed green while the bug returned. The call-site half of that guard is structural and documented as such: the sites sit behind services needing a live Worker, so it protects that the fixes stay fixed and says nothing about their runtime behaviour.

- `IndexCacheService.ResolveHierarchy` and `ListService.ResolveHierarchy` walk the same KB-object `Parent` chain under the same name, and are now documented as deliberately separate. They agree on the walk - skip what is not a Module or Folder, stop at the `DesignModel`, refuse to loop when a parent's `Guid` matches the object's, take the first container as the parent - down to a shape difference with no behavioural content: the index copy hoists the container test into a local and skips ahead, the list copy falls through to the same advance. They part at the end. When the chain yields nothing the index promotes the module into the path, because the result is cached as the index's own identity for the object and that has to stay stable across a cache rebuild; the list copy must not, because its result is reported as `item["parentPath"]` and a module is not where the object lives. A shared helper would pick one and make the other consumer wrong. The reason is recorded on both methods and pinned by `ResolveHierarchyDivergenceTests`, which fails if the promotion is copied across. That guard is source-level and honestly so: the walk gates on `is Module` / `is Folder`, no test in this project builds an SDK Module or Folder, and the half that records a container name is therefore not behaviourally covered here and cannot be without a live KB. What the guard pins is the divergence, not the walk.

- Six duplication candidates were measured and declined, with the reasons recorded so they are not re-proposed. The `catch (Exception ex) { error = ex.Message; return false; }` tail appears 7 times in 6 files, and those are the tails of `Try`-shaped methods in unrelated types with no common base - sharing a one-line catch would be worse than the repetition. `catch { return null; }` appears about 200 times in about 90 files, not the handful first estimated; it is not a shared decision at all but the idiomatic spelling of "best-effort lookup, absent means null", and consolidating it would be actively harmful. `TryPrepareLegacyExport` and `TryPrepareNativeExport` share about 20 lines and then diverge completely, and the part they share is precisely the part that must differ, since one names `.gxtext` files and a manifest and the other a `src`/`ref` tree. The twelve-line `using` block repeated by the three `ObjectTextService` partials could be removed with `global using`, which would make the namespace dependencies of every file in the assembly implicit and ambiguous - trading visible dependencies for hidden ones across the whole project. The six helpers earlier proposed for inlining all have 4- to 12-line bodies rather than the 3 assumed, and each either has five or more call sites or earns its name at its single one.

### Fixed

- A shared Worker is supervised at all. Issue #335: `StartShared(...)` returned before the health task was installed at the end of the isolated startup path, so a shared Worker had **no** supervision - no vitals, no idle reaping, no heap-pressure recycling, no stall detection. It was precisely the Worker the isolation and heap-pressure settings exist for. The health loop is now installed on both startup paths, and the loop can run without a local `Process`: in shared mode the Gateway owns no child process, so liveness is asked of the broker and the child's figures arrive from it. The heartbeat acknowledgement used to be discarded, which is the whole reason there were no vitals - the only party able to answer was being ignored. The broker now reports the child's PID, working set, private and managed heap, queue depths, SDK operation, elapsed time and last-progress time, plus its own supervision generation, on that existing acknowledgement. Extending `heartbeat_ack` rather than adding a frame type keeps this off the protocol-migration path the issue warns about: an older Gateway ignores the new fields and an older broker simply sends none. **Recovery belongs to the broker**, because it owns the child. A Gateway may act only once the broker reports an election, and the broker clears that flag when the new child starts - which is what makes exactly one attachment act per sweep instead of every Gateway watching the Worker deciding to kill it. With no report at all, no recovery decision is taken, because deciding on no evidence is the failure this path exists to remove. **Progressing work is never a recovery target**, whoever owns the child: recycling a progressing build destroys the operation, not just the Worker. Queued work and an unproven lane block recovery too - a busy lane with no observed progress is `busy-unproven` rather than `busy-stalled`, because one sample cannot tell a healthy short call from a deadlocked one. The stall window matches the one `genexus_connection_recover` already uses, so the two paths cannot disagree about the same lane at the same instant. The child-exit and TTL coverage is preserved, and the ownership reconcile stays on the isolated path only, since ownership is this process's own lease rather than a broker-owned child's. Fixes [#335](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/335).

  Not verified, and recorded as unavailable: the issue's two-client disposable-KB smoke (criterion 6) needs a shared broker with real injected child metrics, which this build cannot stage without a shared-mode KB. The synthetic reproductions for criteria 1-5 are covered; the live smoke is not.

### Added

- A reproducible synthetic scale lane now exists, and the numbers it reports are numbers you can act on. Issue #358 asked for a scale lane with measured pass criteria rather than another unmeasured optimization plan. The repository had a 40k benchmark and two live benchmark scripts, and none of the three could gate a change: the benchmark rebuilt its catalog from `Guid.NewGuid()` and `DateTime.UtcNow` on every run, so two runs measured different inputs, and it printed before/after figures with no assertion, no pass/fail and no way to say "could not run". The live scripts drove one KB through one client against a fixed local fixture, so they could not express a 1/3-KB by 1/2-client matrix at all.

  The deterministic half needs no SDK and runs in the ordinary suite (`dotnet test src\GxMcp.Worker.Tests --filter "FullyQualifiedName~SyntheticScaleLaneTests"`). It builds a seeded fictional catalog at 10k/50k/100k objects and drives the real production paths that stop scaling - source-store budget accounting, trigram-index residency under churn, bounded top-K page selection, source search with continuation, and STA admission under bulk load. Every gate is on an operation count, never on wall clock: a CI agent and a developer laptop differ by more than the regressions worth catching, so a fixed millisecond gate fails on hardware and passes on a regression. Timings are recorded in the report and decide nothing. The fixture is fictional on purpose - invented vocabulary, seed-derived GUIDs, a fixed epoch, and a xorshift PRNG rather than `System.Random`, whose sequence is not specified across runtimes and would make the lane irreproducible on the host meant to gate it. Names collide across types because a catalog of globally unique names never exercises the name index's disambiguation path, and each KB index is a distinct catalog so a 3-KB run does not measure one KB twice against a warm cache.

  Three outcomes, never two. `pass`, `fail`, and `unavailable` - and an unavailable run is not a pass, because the alternative is a missing fixture quietly turning green. That distinction is load-bearing: while building this, a criterion reported `-1` for "this never happened" against a limit of `1`, and `-1 <= 1` is true, so the worst outcome in a workload would have passed it. The comparison criterion measures the production comparer itself, so its count is exact on any host; a bounded heap at 100k entries costs ~104k comparisons against the ~5M a growing-list duplicate scan would, which is why the guard separates them rather than merely ranking them. Search completeness is asserted against a hit set known by construction, and a truncated page must be a subset of the complete answer - never a different set, never a repeat, never over its own cap. A faster wrong result does not pass.

  Writing the lane found a real defect. `StaScheduler.Clear()` emptied the three queues but left `_queuedCount` and `_queuedBytes` populated, so `TryEnqueue` kept answering `queue_full` to fresh commands against an empty queue - and the caller has no way to drain a queue that is already empty, so the refusal never self-heals and admission degraded with every clear. No production path called `Clear()` at the time, so nothing was broken in the field; it was a trap, and it is now correct.

  The native half is `scripts/bench-live-http.py`, extended rather than duplicated. `--kbs "path=alias,path=alias"` declares the KB axis - `=` and not `:`, because every Windows KB path starts with a drive letter and a colon separator would be ambiguous - with aliases required to be unique and paths required to be distinct, since the same KB declared twice would let a "3-KB" matrix measure one KB against a warm cache and report it as three. A cell needing more KBs than were declared is emitted as `unavailable` rather than dropped, because a missing cell is indistinguishable from a grid that quietly narrowed itself. `--clients N` opens N independent MCP sessions and, at two or more, additionally measures interactive-read latency while an extra session issues background work: a different question from read latency on an idle worker, and the one that shows whether a bulk operation makes a user-facing call slow. The matrix axes go into the report's `population`, so a comparison cannot pair a 1-KB baseline with a 3-KB current run and report a confident delta between different workloads. A cell that could not run exits 2 rather than 1 - a missing fixture is not a regression, and conflating them is what teaches a reader to ignore red. The matrix layer is pure functions with 30 unit tests, because the native lane is optional precisely because it cannot run everywhere, and behavior only observable when a GeneXus installation happens to be present is behavior that quietly stops being checked.

  Three findings from writing the guards, all now fixed or pinned. First, the budget criterion as originally written could not fail: `CatalogRecordVisits` counts records walked by `SumRecordBytes`, so a total inlined directly into `EnforceStorageBudget` walks no counter, and mutation confirmed the measured criterion stayed green against exactly that regression. The lane now also carries a structural guard, since a measurement that cannot fail for the failure it names is not evidence. Second, `parse_kb_specs` initially accepted the same path twice under different aliases - not caught by the alias check, and exactly the shape that fakes a multi-KB matrix. Third, the fixture originally suffixed every name with its position, so it never produced the cross-type collisions it claimed to exercise; uniqueness is only required *within* a type in a real KB, which is the rule now applied.

  20 deterministic tests plus 29 matrix tests. Eight mutations confirmed red on clean builds: the bounded heap replaced by a duplicate scan, the per-insert catalog rescan, bulk drained ahead of an interactive read, `Clear()` leaving the counters populated, unavailable cells summarizing as a pass, an under-filled cell silently dropped, a missing KB path reporting available, availability never reaching the grid, a malformed spec dropped instead of rejected, and an unusable envelope becoming a latency sample.

  Not run, and recorded as unavailable: no live native measurement. The matrix's measured figures require disposable synthetic KBs, and none is configured on this machine; `C:\KBs` holds real KBs the issue forbids discovering or mutating automatically. The deterministic lane and the matrix logic are fully verified; the native p50/p95 numbers are not, and this entry claims no speedup. Fixes [#358](https://github.com/lennix1337/Genexus18MCP/issues/358).

### Fixed

- A WorkWithPlus apply now reports the objects it actually generated, because it was looking for them under the wrong naming convention. Issue #359: applying WorkWithPlus to `TPacCls` answered `PatternApplied` with `generatedObjects: ["WorkWithPlusTPacCls"]` and nothing else, and the four siblings read back as `ObjectNotFound`. The family lookup probed a hand-written **prefix** list - `WW<Trn>`, `View<Trn>`, `ExportWW<Trn>` - and WorkWithPlus generates a **suffix** family: `<Trn>WW`, `<Trn>WWDS`, `<Trn>WWExport`, `<Trn>WWGetFilterData`, which the issue itself records (`TbandadWW`, `tpaidadww`, `tferdadww`, ...). Every family probe missed.
